PLEA SANT PARAGRAPHS.

A rxrrot boy asked his father what an employ- ment agent is, to which the old gentleman replied: "Why, my son, an employment agent ,is a man who is very anxious to get work for others to do. He himself never wants any." "So," said a lady recently' to an Aberdeen merchant, "your pretty daughter has married a rich husband." " Well,' slowly replied the father, "I believe she has married a rich man, but I un- dersta.nd he is a very poor husband." DOUGLAS JEuRoLD once boasted that a friend of his was the kindest-hearted man in the world. "Why," said the wit, " he is so kind-hearted that he would hold. an umOrella,over.a duck in a shower of rain."

_ A FATHER, who in his college days belonged to the base-ba4ll club, on being asked by his little son what the nine Muses were, replied : " My child, it is when our nine 'is beaten ma game of base-ball. Then the nine muses over it."

IT is reported that an actress in Boston was pro- mised a seal-skin cloak if she wouldn't speak for a quarter of an hour. She set her lips for silence, but at the end of a few minutes she exclaimed : "Mind you: It's got to be a two hundred and tifty dollar one !"

"My son," said a fond father to his little boy, whom he had been punishing by the use of the rod for the first time—" my son, I hope this has taught you a lesson." "Yes, pa," the little boy sob- bingly replied ; "it's talgi.t me that it's better to give than to receive." A LADY of great accomplishments -visiting a man of wit, was much pleased with the display of his talents, and complimented him on them at her de- parture. " Madam,' replied the bard, modestly, " I am only an instrument, and you have shown your skill in playing on me." A FASHIONABLE lady, whose position' in society had been but recently attained, in boasting of he:. new residence remarked that the windows were all of stained glass, whereupon her honest old mother exclaimed: "Why, that's too bad ! It jurct spiles everything! But, won't soap an' iurpentine take the stains out ?"

A CRUSTY old curmudgeon, who was laughing over something in his newspaper, being reproached by his wife for not reading it aloud so she could share in the enjoyment, testily said: "0, fudge, my dear; a thing that's funny enough to make only on laugh would be dreadfully stupid if divided between two."

" Indeed !" responded the lady, sarcastically. " Well, I cannot recall your face, but your nante is familiar ; " and she left him pondering.' A FASHIONABLE lady at an art exhibition severely criticised a painting to one of the artists, who finally told her that he painted the picture himself. 'Wishing "to make things all right," she soothingly said: "0, you mustn't mind what / say. lAm no judge of pictures at all. I only just repeat wt everybody else says, you know !" Poou young artist (displaying a picture)—" That is an exact copy, madame, of Raphael's Madonna, which, you will remember, sold for ,E 25,000. Madame—" And how much do you ask for this picture ?" "Ten shillings and sixpence." "What is the cause of such a difference in price ?" "Com- petition. madame. The business is not what it used to be."

MR. BRowNE, an Irish member, gravely stated in the House of Commons one night that "the Protestant landlords got their lands, subject to tithe, before there were any Protestants at all." This is as good as the " fact " mentioned by the old beef-eater in the Tower, Who, while exhibiting a curious old pistol, declared it to have been made before pistols were invented.

ONE wet, miserable, foggy London day in autumn, Charles Lamb was accosted by a beggar woman with "Pray, sir, bestow a little charity upon a poor destitute widow woman, who is perish- ing for lack of food. Believe me, sir, I have seen better days." "So have I," said Lamb, handing the poor creature a shilling; "so have I; it's a miserable day ! Good-bye! good-bye !" "But, pa, he nevei comes in the morning." "No : I wish he would." "You do ?" "Yes. If he could once see you in the morning he would never come again." IN his latter days, Macklin, from the lair•uor of age, found that he could not call up the violence of passion necessary to five Shylock with due impetu- osity; and it is said that when on the point of rushing on the stage he used to call to the prompter, "Kick my shins! kick my shins !" hoping that the physical pain would supply what was wanting. This is quite characteristic of Macklin.

"who that comical little chap" was. The land- lord told him ; on which the countryman, slapping his thigh with great knowingness, said to a cora- paation, "Dom it, I thowht he was a player. Didn't ye see how he eyed the goose ?"

"DON'T you call this rather muddy ?" asked an English traveller of old Hi Stoga, of Tip Up Junc- tion. " Well, yes," said Hi, as he scraped the mud from his boots with a chip; "this is muddy, but it's nothing to what I saw down on the Twenty- mile Creek, when I was running a mill there. Why, I've hauled logs there with oxen, when all you could see of the cattle was the tips of their horns sticking up through the mud." The English- man silently went on his way. "0. B. A FEMALE teacher was on very intimate terms with the male teacher in the same school. He was

ea the habit of strolling into her room during the

. recess, and chatting with the object of his affec- tions. His name was Smith. One day the lady teacher endeavoured to make the class comprehend the omnipresence of God. She explained to them that God was everywhere. 'Now, my dear chil- Airen, suppose you all go out of this room, except myself, and I stay in here. Am I alone ?" asked the female teacher. "No," exclaimed one of the little girls, " Mr. Smith will be with you."

A PERSIAN author relates that a lover was searching every piece for a good physician to prescribe to his mistress who was ill. He met a person who possessed a talisman which enabled the possessor to see ghosts. On looking in it, he saw crowds of ghosts go about the door of one physican, who had formerly been his patients; several others though not so many. about the door of another doctor; at last, at one door he saw only a single _ghost. He immediately called on the doctor, and with many compliments to his skill and superior practice, desired his advice. "My superior practice," cried the doctor, "why, I never had but two patients in my life." "CAPITAL " CHAT.

It must not be imagined that the latest Anarchist pronouncement, that " all means, however desper- ate, are justifiable " in order to accomplish what is described as " the emancipation of labour throug:lout the world," commands the sympathy of a united Anarchist body. The authors of this pronouncement themselves admit that it is well to " subordinate " the idea of force, whilst the econ- omic Anarchists denounce the idea with relentless vigour, and announce their intention to oppose it with all the power at their command. They held aloof from the recent conference at which the An- archist Communists formulated their pronounce- melit, though they had originally intended taking part in it. They now meditate a conference crt their own, and at this conference they will com- plete arrangements for an active propaganda, one of the purposes of which will be to convince audi- ences in all parts of the kingdom that Anarchism is an econ3mic faith which does not require the support of eutrage. ,

The report of the French pisciculturist M. Jousset de Bellesmas deserves to be carefully studied by those who wish to increase the numbers of edible fish in our inland waters. It is a i:eport remarkable for its singleness of aim. M. de Belles- mes simply desires to propagate fish which would furnish delicate food. Our own pisciculturists are too often distracted between the wish to provide food and the wish to provide sport. Thus they pour trout into waters teeming with coarse fish, with the result that most of the trout succumb in the struggle for existence. Coari.e fish give humble anglers in the neighbourhood of large towns infinite pleasure, but there is no reason why edible fish should not more assiduously be cultivated in remoter waters. Then, again, M. de Bellesmes has been active in promoting the acclimatisation of new species and varieties, and seems to have been parti- cularly successful with Californian salmon. Here, since the introduction of the black bass some years ago—and we hear little of that fish nowadays,— our pisciculturists have been singularly languid in making these useful and inieresting experiments. It should, however, be added that both in France and America pisciculture is regarded as Govern- ment business, whereas with us—exqp.pt as regards the inspection of salmon rivers—it is left to the desultory efforts of individuals.

The "1131 y carpet " of the mosque at Ardebil which is one of the finest of the delicate velvety piled goat hair carpets made in Persia during the sixteenth century, is exhibited now in the first division or compartment of the gallery connecting the Indian art and scientific apparatHs collectiou at South Kensington Museum. It is a pity that this exceptional example of beautiful pattern, de- sign,and blending of colour is so far removed from correspondingly important type specimens of in- dustrial art which are in the main buildings of the South Kensington Museum, on the other side of the rcad. However, the carpet will be well lighted in rh-e place it occupies, and capable of yielding valu- able lessons t) those who want them, though many visitors to the South Kensington treasures will have some difficulty in running it to ground in the laby- rinthine corridors constituting the larger part of the Museum premises. The name of the weaver, " Nlalcsoud, of Koshan," and the date All. 046 (A.D.

1540), are woven, with an inscription, in a panel to- viarCs the centre of one of the narrower ends of tile carpet. The Museum authorities found £1,750 of the purchase money, amounting to £2.500, mud the balance was subscribed by Mr. Franks, C. 8., Mr. StenkopiT, Mr. Wm. Morris, and others.

Truly, Libour is king. Here we have a sprig of " Our Old Nobilitee," Lord Willoughby, desperatel., endeavouring to persuade the electors of Horncastle that he is their true and only friend. His support- ers have facetiously dubbed him " the popular candidate,' and he is in line with the hated and re- viled Newcastle pr•gramrne in almost every parti- cular— with a difference which the toilers ot Lin- colnshire cannot fail to perceive. Lord Willoughby loves parish councils—he has always believed in them—but he objects to some points in the Govern- ment Bill, and he, therefore, cannot support it. All the Tories are obliged to be hypocritical after this fashion. I hey favour every one of the Liberal re- forms in the abstract. They obstruct the effort to put them into practice in the Commons : and wb.it: obstruction tails, they reject them or render them worthless in the House of Lords, where Toryism as it really is cannot help displaying itself.

Mr. Knatchbull-Hugessen's new year has not be- gun happily. He has read Lord Willough hy's elec- toral address. The plain truth is that the Tories occupy an indefen.tible position, and they know it. Good, honest, robust,uncompromising Conservatism of the type of professed by Mr. Klldtabull-Hugeo- sen and Mr.S•Ainley Leighton can be readily under- stood and appreciated. But the piebald Radicalism of the lip which passes muster as Toryism in thy constituencies is entitled to neither respect or sup- port. No man, no matter how stupid, ever started off on a career of humbug without finding some oelievers. There are very many able men engaged in propagating the present Tory humbug. They may be more or less successful for a time, but trut will eventually prevail. Unhappily, no useful pur- pose would be served by getting Lord Willoughby to give us his idea of what a Pari:h Councils Bili ought to be. He does not know.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, JANUARY 6. 1894. LADIES' LETTER.

A custom which extends all over JApan is that of tak- ing your " boy " with you when you go out to dine. He waits at table and performs any needed service as if he belonged to the house. Lady Griselda Ogilvie, sister of the Earl of Airlie, is going through a course of training in Edinburgh to qualify for a nurse. She is at present a probationer in the Children's Hospital. The following notice was posted up on a French pleasure-boat :—" The chairs in the cabin are for the ladies. Gentlemen are requested not to make use of them till the ladies are seated."

It is said that Edisan, who is not a man to be influenced by any sentimental considerations, has the highest opinion of women workers, ancfl employs them in his laboratories and workshops wherever possible. A young woman in Covington, Kentucky, is dying because she can't stop laughing at a joke cracked at the dinner table. Miss Pruett, of Philadelphia, died recently by bursting a blood-vessel while laughing at a witticism. A funny story is told of Miss Fortescue in regard to the first time she played Juliet in London. The fair actress possesses beautiful eyes,but they are shortsighted ones, and she cannot see beyond the second row of the stalls. On this p4rticular night she got so excited in the poison scene, that she actually threw the phial at the head of a well known critic seated in the stalls.

Mdme. Carnot strives to be the best-dressed woman in France, and with Felix, her favourite couturier, she has frequent consultations. Her desire to be well dressed in not a passion for dress, but a wish to do everything is keeping with her position. She might Ise pedantic, for her bookiearning is marvellous,but she is very unaffected. Not a Frenchwoman understands better than Mdme• Carnot the political situation of E•irope, and not one devotes herself more sincerely to the welfare of France. Sarony, the great New York photographer, says that 13ernhardt's figure is " not the apothesis of per- fection," but as an artist lie wa4 "charmed with her pulchritudinous personality.'" As if this were not ssartling enough, there is "a sinuous grace in her move- ments, a Hogarthean curve in her poses." Wishing to photograph Bernhardt as Leah, Sarony requested her to expose her bare feet. "She had on stockings with fingers for the toes," he said, "but I would not have them,"

To Clean Burnt Saucepans. Half fill the saucepan with cold water. Cut into the cold water a piece ot soap the size of a walnut.put on the lid and lot the saucepan stand whc,re it will boil gently for an hour ; let it get rather cool before cleaning,then take a scrubbing Crush, scrub well, rinse, and dry in the usual way.

Rabbit Pudding. Line a pudding-basin with a crust made with two ounces of dripping, chopped into six ounces of flour ; Cu! into neat pieces half a pound of pickled pork or bqcon, lay two Qr three pieces at the bottom of the lined pud- ding basin. Cut the rabbit, when well washed, into neat joirts ; lay on the bacon. When all the rabbit is in, put in tne rcnnainder of the slices of pork, with a tea- spoonful of chopped parsley and onion, pepper, and a heaped tablespoonful of flour ; till up with boiling water, put on the lid of paste, tie a pudding-cl,th over the top, set in a saucepan three parts full of boiling wate '- boil three hours ; serve in the basin.

Double Knitting for Boy's or Girl's Scarf. Cast en any even number of stitchos, allowing two or three stitches at fiach end of the row for cage stitches, steel needles, and any soft, pretty-coloured wool that YOU may fancy. Cast on twelve for the pattern, four extra for the edge stitches ; the first two rows plain knitting, third row knit two plain.knit one, putting the wool twice round the needle instead of once, bring wool forward as though you were going to purl, slip the stitch put the wool tack. Knit one, putting the wool twice round the needle ; repeat to thz, end of the twelve stitches, knit the last two plain. Third row, knit twt plain ; knit one, putting the wool twice round the needle, bring wool forward, slip the stitch, allowing the extra lo,:p to Shp off ; put wool back, rr.peat to end ot row ; every row is the same. When the right length knit two rows plain and cast off loosely. For a boy,make a fringe by putting two 4-inch lengths of wool into every stitch of the commencement and finish of scarf. For a girl, take a crochet hook, pick up all the loops on to a double strand of wool, draw them all together and tie securely. Crochet six chain and make a tassel at the, end by winding the wool round a 3-web length of card- board, slip it off at the twentieth round, pass the hook through, fasten at the top and then round the middle to make a tassel.

A Small Income.

To manage a small income well, it is quite as neces- sary to give thought aad care in order to spend well, as it is to give thought to saving. The less money we have the more we need to get the most out of it, and the more we ought to be afraid to buy cheap things because they are chcap. Because we have little to spend, we ought to pause, consider and reflect before we lay out even a penny, so that. we may lay that penny out to the best advautog, on this account it is a help to go to a good shop, and also to buy of such things as will keep, a quantity at a time, and not in small parcels. It is par- ticularly hard, that the people who have the least money . usually pay the highest price for the things they bay, and because they buy in driblets they get goods of infer- ior quality. If you could imagine such a thing as that a housekeeper had to buy a pound of tea in farthings' worths, we might be perfectly sure that she would pay fully twice as much for it as if she bought it by the pound. By all means, therefore, a mother who has to practice strict economy should try to arrange her pur- ChASPS so as to buy in quantity (of such things as wi t keep), and to buy of good quality. Also sue should determine not to give way to prejudice, and fancy that everything that is new is poor. There are in these days new ways of living, new sorts of food, and new methods of cookery, to be met with 'An all sides, and the only persons who will never gain any benefit from them are the persons woo scorn them and need them most- The problem of " How to Manage on a Small Income " is a very difficult one ; no one knows how difficult it is excepting those who have tried it. Also the most valu- able suggestions on the subject are to be gathered from those who have learnt from experience. It is well worth while, therefore, when we have the opportunity, to study carefully the methods which have been adopted by people who have mastered the problem. Fortunately in this instance actual experience can be studied by all. " Cassell's Book of the Household."

pressed, gleaming light in his eyes. "On the slightest provocation I will challenge the man you love to mortal combat," he cried, fiercely, "and he shall kill me—or I shall kill him." With those words he turned and left her, and she had cause to remember them until the day she died.

"He is no gentleman who dares threaten a lady I" cried an angry voice from behind ; but Captain Lansing paid no heed save to quicken his pace. He recognised the voice as Rutledge Chester's, and he knew if he paused a single instant there would be a tragedy at the fete. "Pardon me, Verlie," cried Rutledge, coming hurriedly up the path and taking the little, trembling, ice-cold hands in his as he bent over her; "I was coming up the path, and I could not help overhearing that cowardly remark. Think no more of it, I beg you." The lovely dimpled face, was as white as the snow-white blossoms she wore on her breast, and the lovely blue-bells of eyes were swimming in tears.

She was trembling so violently he clasped her little hands still closer and drew her down the rose-bordered path to the rustic seat beneath the magnolia tree, and seated her upon it. "You sent him away, Verlie. Was it because you cared for any one else ?" be whispered, cling- ing firmly but gently to the little white hands that were struggling, like fluttering, imprisoned birds, to free themselves from his clasp. But she did not answer him. He could see a burning flush creep over the girl's beautiful, dimpled face, and a strange thrill shot through his heart. Was Heaven to give him the desire of his heart at last? he whispered to himself. "Look up into my face and tell me if it is so, dear," he said, gently, "and that will give me hope.'' She attempted to fly from him in pretty, girlish confusion and dismay. She would not look at him —no, that she would not for worlds—for he would be sure to read the secret of her love for him in her eyes. "My timid, beautiful Verlie !" he cried, holding the lii.tle hands fast, despite her pleadings that he should release her at once, for her guests would miss her.

"I shall not release you until you have answered my question, dear," he said, his voice thrilling with eager expectancy. "Was it because you cared for some one else—for me 1"

Only the night winds and the handsome, happy lover knew her answer; but it must have been satisfactory, for the old, old story was told again that young hearts always respond to and old hearts remember with memories tender —the story of love's young dream. When Rutledge parted from Verlie that night he raised her white hand to his lips, whispering, tenderly : "Heaven has granted me the one yearning desire of my life, Verlie ! You love me! You are to be my bride !" Flow little either of them dreamed what the dark future held in store for them, or how it was to end! Ah if they had but known, how much misery might have been spared them!

CHAPTER XXVIII.

THE RIVAL LOVERS.

"The desire of my heart is granted, Verlie," said Rutledge Chester, tenderly. "You have promised to be my bride, but there is one promise I must exact of you, and that is, that you will, from this time forth, discourage the attentions of Captain Lansing." "You must not commence by being jealous of me," said Verlie, with an irresistible pout of her rosebud lips. "I —could not endure a jealous lover." "I am jealous, my darling. I cannot help it," he declared, frankly. "Jealousy runs in my blood. It is the curse of the Chest&s. It has always been a matter of life and death with them. It has caused duels bitter and fierce. But there, I must not frighten you, my darling." "You must trust me, Rutledge," returned Ver- lie, earnestly, "because lam betrothed to you. I cannot be rude to others, now can I?" "If you smile upon any one save myself I shall feel !ike taking you away from him instantly," he said. "I could not bear to see that kind of thing going on. I should long to take your hand before them all, and say : She is mice. Flatter, woo, covet as much as you will, she is mine; you have not the least chance of winning her.' I have a special aversion toward Captain Lansing. If you wish to please me, Verlie, do not dance with him again to-night." "I think I may safely promise that, for he left me in anger as you came up, you know. lam quite sure he has left the villa." "So nmch the better," declared Rutledge; but his satisfaction was premature, for, raising his eyes, he saw the captain hurriedly approaching, his face quite as calm as though nothing out of the order of events had transpired. He had fled from the grounds in hot haste; but as he reached the arched gateway he experienced quite a revulsion of feelings. "Why should I leave the field so completely to any foe?" he asked himself, fiercely. No, he would not. Vedic, had refused him. Still, "faint heart never yet won fair lady." He remembered. Verlie had promised to waltz with him. Why not claim it in spite of all? With the impulsive captain, to think was to act. He turned on his heel and sauntered back in- to the grounds again, searching eagerly among the merry, chattering throng for Verlie. At last he beheld her standing under a blossoming magnolia tree.

MT DEAREST NELLIE,

Silk appears to be returning to favour again, and you will find almost all are shot, the colourings being novel and very brilliant. Mori6 silks are made with a coarser rib, and they are produced in very beautiful mixtures of colour. Satins are quite as much worn as ever, and at some of the ler e shops they have a very magnificent reversible satin. Iwo colours, of course, are used—green has violet on the other side, gold colour has emerald green, and dark blue has green or gold. Silken gowns and velveticostumes are unquestion- ably admirable, specially when trimmed with lace or fur, as fashion dictates. Bat theoe are not sui .A to play their part on muddy pavements and beneath fur coats; while, as a matter of fact, it is a question open to doubt whether there is any dress, any style more becoming to the figure than a plain tight cloth gowa when it is cut by an ex pert. Experience has taught him that women do not watt their clothes too tight, and the artificially small waist is not the ultimathule of womankind, and some few_tailors I know do contrive the new capelik.e basques and outspreading skirts most Per- fectly.

At the prczent season the qw3stion ot how you shall dress your hair will possibly trouble you. A headdress carelessly waved and frizzed, and orna- mented in the centre with twists and soft rolls is very pretty.

Partaking ef the nature of an ulster, and yet re- joicing in the modern improvements suggested by the pelisse, is a long coat with voluminous folds and a pointed pelerine edged with for. Further- more, you may find tweed dresses are of singular attractiveness, cloth coats, long and short, plain and trimmed, and you will (,bserve, should you ba a woman of discernment, how admirable are alike the cut and workmanship in many seasonable cos- tumes.

Striking, is costume of dark blue Venetian cloth, on which the strapping of the seams is lifted into a high art, so neatly are they stitched and pressed ; and not alone on the bodice and sleeves are they strapped, but on the skirt also. And let me des- cribe the bodice. It fastens on the chest with but one button, and it has a full baEque, deftly set into one pleat in the centre of the back; the sleeves are set into three box pleats on the shoulder, 'While the collar is faced with velvet, and the waistcoat shows a blue and white and red Tattersall.

All manner of dainty fichu and ruffle arrange- ments are the fashion now; they may be worn either in the afternoon or evening, and are most becoming. A pretty evening fichu to wear over a velveteen or black silk gown is of pale pink mous- seline chiffon, the front and back are gathered in soft tucks into a round yoke piece of soft white Lace, and form to a point back and front; the outer edge is trimmed with a frill of lace the frill wide and very full over the shoulders, graduates to quite a narrow width at the points; the neck is finished with a soft lace ruffle, the front point is attached to a folded band of pale pink satin; it fastens at the beck in a soft bow. For brightening up a dark-eoloured dress in the afternoon the Marie Antoinette fichu, composed of cream-coloured spotted net, is very effective. It gathers in soft folds over the bust, the outer edge being trimmed with a deep double frill of the net; the ends are erossed at the waist in front, and hang loosely over the dress; this style of fichu in black spotted net looks well over a light-coloured dress in the evening. Winter bonnets are small as to shape. They lie flat and close on the head, and are trimmed with full 'bows at the back of the crown and smaller ones in the front. The shapes and trim- mings are mostly in black, with a relief of 'colour deftly introduced in the shape of ribbon bows to fasten, as it were, the black feathers in their place. A quiet, but stylish toque has a crown of black astrachan lamb, and a brim of heliotrope velvet, the trimming consisting simply of a big bow of velvet, and a large paste btickle at the side. But the majority of people adhere to the old style of toque with velvet crown and fur brim, and an exceedingly pretty model is of shot dark green and gold velvet, with sable brim and garniture of gilt- headed pins and green osprey e ingled with loops of velvet. Felt toques are useful, although they are scarcely so smart-looking, and many have. crowns of fawn or drab felt, brims of black velvet, and trimmings of black wings and pink or pale blue velvet rosettes. Nearly all toques, by the way, are inclined to be equare in front rather than pointed or rounded.

A pretty jacket !a of faced cloth with a long- ishaped pelerine of the same material, edged with mink, which fur is repeated on the gauntlet cuffs. A tiay 111 trie Sr hart bonnet of green velvet, edged with jet pailetres, is adorned at the back with a sabh- tail and a jet ai;rette. Should the jacket be maCe af thick cloth, a lining is quite unnecessary; but etch seam must be neatly finished, and bound with Prussia binding.

On this eventful evening a slim flgure, wearing a long dark travelling cloak, her face concealed by a thick veil, had stepped off the southern express that had steamed into the gay capital. It was beautiful, hapless Uldene. "I am mad to come here," she murmured, brokenly, under her sobbing breath. "But I must see him! I must look upon his face again, or die !" "It is two years since that fatal railway accident occurred in which he believed I had lost my miser- able life. Ah l would to Heaven that I had I" "While he, my young husband,, has been travel- ling abroad, seeking in vain to look calmly upon life without me, how has it fared with me?' "How I used to. laugh in madame's face at boarding school when she used to say, 'lf you are ever called upon to battle with the world, Uldene, you can earn your bread by giving music lessons. Ah, child! you are a genius in music." In the darkest hour of Uldenes life those words came back to her—when she found herself alone, friendless and penniless, thrown upon her own resources in a cold, hard, pitiless world. It was no easy task securing pupils with all her skill, and many a time Uldene was reduced to positive want—she who had known every luxury, every pleasure. In hard work alone she sought to drown the yearning cry of her soul for Rutledge. Oh, if she could but look upon him just once more, she could go away forever, her heart at peace. How often she thoupt of the pitiful lines that seemed almost to have been written especially for her : "Only to see his face again, full of beauty and of grace ; One little prayer—'tie all I ask—only to see his face." Then Uldene picked up the morning paper one nay, and read among the personals the return of Rutledge Cheater, and that he would spend a few weeks in Washington, she raised her face to the smiling, sunlit heavens, crying out : " I cannot be so near him without looking on his, face just once—only once!: Then I will go quietly away again, and live my lonely life out. My starved hearo will be tia,tiatied." - She had nest that her huThand was stopping at Willaid's Hotel, and thither she bent her steps. Just as she was abreast of the entrance, two gentle- • men hurriedly passed her and entered a coach in , waiting. One of heta she recognized in that fizet- jag glimpse as Rutledge—her Rutledge. She did not cry out or iii.tec any moan, but her j,,y was so 1,.;r01t, at be'nelding him that it made her faint and dizzy. "To the Renw.cli illa, A Avenue, Number—,"' she heard him say. Ah, yes ! she reznemberA the place well. She sti)od quite inotion:eirl, gazing with tear- blinded eyed it tee the rapidly disappearing coach. Por Uldene. ! in her pitiful love for him, she could have knc:t: down and kissed the cold pave- nicht over which his dear ieet had passed. (To lie cop tinned. )

now BEGGARS ARE TREATED IN SAN FRANCISC,IO.

The " Argonaut " says :—ln San Francisco, as in fil,:st other (nth s just now, beggars are becoming a seri- nuisance. Ia the residence quarters the bt-ll is kept ro.q.:ing all day long by tramps in various degrees of raggedness, who are ready to receive anything from a (nine to a slice of bread and butter. Welt dress-1 per- seks are constantly accosted on the streets by wa3 Were*. •,v:iose apiiearance is prima-facie evidence that tney are out of work. Benevolent householders have, in some cases, provided a special larder for beggars, and let no one leave their doors unrelieved. It does not occur to t.ilere kindly-disposed persons that they are encouraging inendicity and improvidence,with a pet spectivr of crime. Yet they must perceive, on reflection, that 4; tunaPB can pick up a living by beggary, their incentive •o go to work is deStroyid. The business of outcoer relief is rTt or:ailised in San Francisco as it is.in some other cities. a beginning has been macie by wresting the Sand-lot out of the hands of Willey and Fry ana that gang, and i,lacing it under the control of the Salvation Army. It n,v administered by Major M'Pliee. Hero every hungry tramp can get two square meals a day, of meat, 6up, and bread, and a night's lodging,. LlA.rely by stat- ing that he is out of work, hungry and homeless. The ,N stem has been working long enough to enable the n.n wets to reach certain conclusiens. ,Of the tramps who are relieved, about one-third: are really destitute 101.11 who are looking for work,,and are dest,tute because they cannot find it ; one-thiro are men who would work provided they can dictate terms and conditions and the nature of tlie work suits them ;:the retnaioing third con- sists of protesional tramps woo would ri,at work at all under any conditions. The actions of the two latter classes have been observed. After their breakfast and sivoke at the Sand-lot, they wander into the residence qwrter arid beg from house to house. They do not care tor crusts of bread ; if such coarse hood is minded them they drop it into the gutter as soon as the donor is out of sight. What they want is money or choice articles of food. With these in their waliet they repair to the Sand- tot, and with the aid of a few chips and chunks of coal, they cook themselves a nice hot lunch, which stays their appetite till the hour for the plain Salvation Army sup- tier. The officer of the Salvation Army in charge at. the Sand-lot does not considtr. ic advisable to continue the system of out-door relief there, and it is to be stopped. 'the " unemployed " must be dealt with otherwise.

111 E AIVIINER, SATURDAY. JANUARY 6, 1894. IMPERIAL PARLIAMENT.

Alderman Surrox was pleased to see the members manifest such a spirit of economy. It was quite a new feature on their part. (Laughter.) He had no strong feeling one way or the other about the scheme, but he saw nothing to he alarmed about because the resolution simply " invited " drawings. If they commenced to al- ter and alter without a proper schemc before them, they would be working in the oark. Councillor DAVIES thought the matter might be shelved. , altogether. If plans were prepared now they would be useless 20 years hence, and money would thus be wasted. Councillor EOBERTS asked, seeing the Council in June decided to advertise for competitive drawings with a • premium, was it necessary that the resolution should be brought up again that night? The TOWN CLERK replied that the resolution was not acted upon. The Council would notice the word in the , present resolution that the drawings be "now" invited. Councillor ROBERTS said the'rown Hall wall was to be ' taken down, and the majority of the members thought that would be an improvement. The very first result would be that the Council would be compelled,for mere decency's sake, to provide accommodation for the horses now stabled on the premises. .("Quite right" and "No, no.") At present the stables were occupied by horses belonging to two ccminittees. Mr. Wright had suggested stables at Howley, and the Sanitary 'Works Committee would probably be compelled to erect another stable. Every business man would know that such an arrange- ment would be a mistake. Businessmen required consoli- dation of their forces if they wished to practioa economy. 'The necessity for a police court and a brideveell had been mentioned, and the Council would have to consider, in the interests of economy, whether they would go in for a consolidation of their public buildings or spread them piecemeal in all quarters of the borough. If the resolu- tion was adopted that night the only expense would be the premiums. and he could say without any doubt that with the knowledge possessed by architects now a scheme could be got which would suffice for the require- ments of Warrington for the next 100 years. If neces- sary they could proceed as the funds and needs of the borough permitted. He admitted that tiro rates for the present year were heavy,but if they averaged the rates of the last ten years they would find that they were less than for the ten years previeus. He admitted that this was an inopportune time to spend from £25,000 to £30,000, and he would not support the resolution if he thought it was contemplated to carry out the scheme immediately. Councillor SMETHURST pointed out 'that there were other improvements demanding attention far more than this one. He objected to improvements being carried 'int piecemeal. 'iouncillor SHAW GREEN said accommodation was re- quired for the staff they had at the Tawu Hall. A num- ber of officials were at present working in a room which was a committee room, and which should be 'used as such. He was, however, willing to postpone the ques- tion fora few months.

s Alderman HARRISON remarked that if they .contem- ,plated the demolition of the present building and the erection of new, he could understand•the need for draw- ings, but when they had a good Town Hail that would answer their purposes for many years, with a few addi- tions, he failed to see that the money proposed to be spent would be well spent. Counsillor PLIMPTON supported the amendment. 'He thoug,htlthat if the resolution objected to was carried it would be simply throwing £175 away. He very much liked the idea of Alderman Wright iturning the present etables into offices, and of taking the,horses and tubs to Longford. He had heard it said that they-could not im- prove that Town Hall without pulling it dow'n altogether grid, for his part, he thought it was a very good Town Hall. .(Hear, hear.) The voting was then proceeded with, snd resulted as follows : .

For the amendment—Aldermen Ashton, Harrison, Wright, Blackhurst, Francomb ; Councillor: Bolton, Flood, Longton, Muir, Plumpton, Oarner, Wilkinson, Beaman;Shaw. Bennett, Davies, Skelton. Shillcock, Smethurst, Hill, Greening, Cannell-22. Against— Alderman Sutton, Councillors Hepherd, Heaton, J.llfair- dough, R. T. Fairclough, Monks, Roberts-7. The CHAIRMAN.—I declare the amendment carried. The minutes were then passed with the exception of the resolution relating t , the drawings.

THE ALLSSACR,E OF CATHOLICS IN IN RUSSIAN POLAND.

HORRIBLE CRUELTY OF TI3E COSSACKS. The ••!Koelnische Volkzeitung," a Roman Catholic organ. publishes from a trustworthy source somoticken- ing details of the massacre of Krosche, in the Govern- ment ofiZovno, in Russian Poland. At two o'cleck•on the ma:ning ot the 10th Novemter, Prefect Klingeriberg,. of Kovno, arrived at Krosche, accompanied by forty strongly-armed policemen. There were about seventy personsin the church. Cursing and yelling, the poliae rushed atthe• -devotees, beat them with the knout. And. struck them with their swords, so that the -church echoed with the cries of the wounded. Some succeeded ' in fleeing to the belfry, where they rang the bells, thus summoning • the. rest of the inhabitants, who crowdedH into the building. •When the day began to wane,,Zoo. Cossacks arrived at the place, armed with rises., lane es,: and knouts. The Cossacks' knouts have wire and iron twisted in at the end, so that every stroke tears :the flesh. Theyidivided themselves into two groups., and a great massacre began. The blood of the defenceless in-. habitants who.were trying to protect their sanctuary stained the walls: of the church and flowed on the oor. Some of the Cossacks ,flew at the altars, smashed the crucifixes, candlesticks, and images of the saints, and dashed them on the ground. .The broken vessels and , images were ttbrown into a cesspool. Some corpses found in tha,church were tossed by the Cossacks into the litnepit iii the churchyard. The people, who Iltml in all directions, were pursued by the Cossacks and taken prisoners. Many sprang into the swollen river aud were drowned. The rest -were surrounded by Cossacks and , driven by them to • the front of the Town Hall on the Market-place. Every inhabitant of Krosche was to :re- iceive a special punishment. A doctor was fetched, and was to say how many -strokes with the knout each vie,- tim could bear. They were brought up singly,and their clothes tern off their bodies. They were then made to lie down naked between two rows of Cossacks, and rtogged with the ineut. 'Nobody was spared, hot even , delicate women. This inhuman work lasted till sun- down, when the pear wretches were taken off to prison. Then the Prefect a2lowed his Cossacks to " enjoy them- selves."

iCARRUTHERS & LANCASTER.

FRIDAY

In the House of Commons on Friday evening,Mr.GLAD- STONE in answer to Mr. Labouchere, said it was not at present deemed desirable to add to the statements which had already been made on behalf of the Government with regard to the occupation of Egypt. Any negotiations would have to take place, not with the Khedive and his advisers, but with the suzerian Power. The House‘again went into Committee on the Local Government (Parish Councils) Bill. After along discus- sion and a protest from Mr. Balfour, clause 19, relating to the qualifications of poor-law guardians, was added to the bill. Clause 20 was also passed before the House rose.

===== 0002601_18940106_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
MONDAY.

In the House of Commons on Monday,Mr. GLADSTONB in reply to questions, said he thought it would be practicable to send the Local Government Bill to the House of Lords by the 19th inst., at the latest without resorting to exceptional measures. Opportunity, he remarked, would be given within the present sittings of Parliament for discussion on report with regard to Featherstone disturbances. _

The House then went into Committee on the Local Government Bill, and considered clause 21, which pro- vided that the chairman of a district council should, by virtue of his office, be a justice of the peace for the county in which the district was situated. Mr. STUART- WORTLEY moved an amendment that the chairman should become a justice of the peace only with the approval of the Lord Chancellor. The amendment was negatived. Mr. FOWLER proposed an amendment that a woman should not be qualified to be a justice, and after lively debate it was carried by 144 votes to 38. On the ques- tion that the clause as attended should stand part of the bill, Mr. HANBURY objected. There would, lie said, he as many magistrates as policemen in the county of Lancaster if the principle of popular election were adopted. A long discussion followed, and the clause was finally adopted by 144 votes to 65. Clauses 22 and 23, after amendment were added Ito the bill. Much dis- sussion followed, chiefly on the question of the control of highways, and the House adjourned at midnight.

===== 0002601_18940106_art0027.txt | included=true | included =====
TUESDAY.,

In the House of Commons on Tuesday evening Mr. GLADSTONE, replying to Mr. Forwood, said the Brazilian ingurgents had not been recognised as belligerents, but. the naval forces at Rio could not protect British mercan- tile ships from the risks which must attend the landing of cargoes while the two contending parties were firing at each other. Viscount CR.ANBORNE, in interrogating the Vice-Presi- dent of the Council on the subject of a charge for books in schools, asked whether the effect of a certain decision of the Education Department did not seriously diminish the resources of schools. Mr. Acland said that if the principle on which the Department had acted had inter- fered with the resources of some schools he was afraid that was the inevitable result of the Free Education Act. The right hon. gentleman, in reply to another question, said he thought physical exercises should be part of the ordinary curriculum and organisation of every school, and he was considering the matter in connection with next year's Code. The House again went into Committee on the Local Gevernment (Parish Councils) till. Several dames were gone through. On clause 29, an amendment was agreed to, which was moved by Mr. Fowler, declaring that the Local Government Board might, on the application of the council of any municipal borough or urban district, nialv, an order giving such body power to appoint overseers and assistant overseers, and discharge the powers, duties, and liabilities of a parish council, this provision to apply to the administrative county of London.

===== 0002601_18940106_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
WEDNESDAY.

Tbe House of Commons on assembling en Wednesday at once went into Committee on the Local Governmeat Bill, and discussion was proceeded with of clause 31, which relates to the register of parochial electors. Mr. FOWLER proposed an amendment laying down the con- ditions on which the revision of the voting lists should be carriecl out, and he said a question for the House to face was whether they should make temporary arrange- ments so as to make the bill operate this year or let the matter stand over blithe next registration was complete He hoped that when they got the clause 60 or 61 to sub- mit a temporary scheme which would meet the difficulty without involving great additional exi. ense. The amend- ment was, after some discussion, agreed to, and the clause was added to the bill. Mr. FOWLER accepted an amendment to clause 32 that six parish electors should have power to summon a parish meeting,and that clause also was added to the bill. Clauses 33 to 44 were also got tin-onel before the House rose.

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD. ITEM MURNALIsT AND THE

The monthly meeting was held at the Town Hall, on Tuesday night. Mr. James Pritchard presided and there were present the Rev. Canon Maitland Wood,Dr. McDougall, Mr. E. Imi3on,and Mr.G. Moss, clerk

===== 0002601_18940106_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
SCHOOL ATTENDANCE.

The School Attendance Committee held a meeting on December 19th, when 32 parents were summoned be- fore the committee, and 24 appeared. It was recom- mended that when the Attendance Officer finds that he is losing a prosecution at the Police Court for want of evidence, he shall apply for an adjournment until next Court day, and in Use interval shalt obtain the required evidence, by witness summons if necessary.—The minutes were approved on the motion of Canon Wood, seconded by Mr. Imison.

===== 0002601_18940106_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
THE STANDARD OF EXEMPTION.

The Clerk read a circular letter from the Education Department, stating that on and after the Ist January the minimum age of exemption from attendance at school would be eleven years. The circular has been previously published.--It was stated by the Clerk that the alteration would have no effect in Runcorn, because there was no half time employment, and because the standard of exemption was so high, the sixth standard, that no child could possibly leave before eleven years of age.

===== 0002601_18940106_art0034.txt | included=true | included =====
THE PROPOSED SCHOOL EXTENSION.

A communication was read from Messrs. F. and G. Holme, of Liverpool, with reference to the extension of Greenway•road school. The firm enclosed a plan of the proposed new classroom, teachers room, and exten- sion of lavatories. The probable cost of the new class rooms and teachers' room would be £450, and for the extension of lavatory of about £so.—Dr. McDougall moved that the plan be approved and that the clerk be instructed to forward it to the Education Department. He said the alterations would , meet a want which had been felt by the School Attend- ance Committee for sometime and they were undertaken on the recommendation .of that Committee. There would be an additional accommodation for 87 girls. The schools would be completed as originally intended to be and would therefore be a considerable improve- ment. Canon Wood said he noticed from the last return that there was accommodation for over 500 scholors more than were in average attendance. He did not know whether the matter was finally closed but he thought they ought to consider how far the alterations were actually necessary. In reply to Mr. unison, the Canon said the return showed considerable accommoda- tion for girls. The Clerk said the accommodation was largely for boys. The total falling off in the average attendance for girls had only been 30 during the whole time the Ship Canal had been completed.—Canon Wood said he believed the population would diminish consider- ably now that the Ship Canal was finished. The Chair- man said bet month's return showed accommodation in excess of average attendance of 537 places, but it only shcwed an excess of 53 places for girls. The extra accommodation was for boys and infants. In Green- way-road school there were 36 girls in average atten- vance in excess of accommodation and this number de- ducted from 53, left 17 places. The Clerk : And that re- turn is for December when the attendance is bad.—The Chairman said taking the number on the rolls,the return showed a great need of accommodation of girls.—Canon Wood said he was not in any way opposed to providing the necessary accommodation, but there was such a curious conjunction of circumstances, and it would not be wise to add considerably to the accommodation for girls, when nobody could be quite certain that in Run- corn there would not be a good deal more surplus than there was at the present time. What he thought ought to be noticed was the great increase of the surplus within the last month or two.—The Clerk: It is the falling off in the percentage of attendance that makes the differ- ence.—Dr. McDougall said there would be no harm in adopting the plans. He should be very much opposed to expending any money in extensions if it were found not to be necessary.—Canon Wood : It may possibly turn out in the next three or four months that there will be a considerable diminution in population. I feel sure there will be, and we are just going to do a thing at a doubtful moment.—The Clerk said should the board decide to carry out the alterations they could not possi- bly be done before the July holidays.—Mr. Imison : The subject is bound to come before us aaain.—Canon Wood I shall be glad to second the motion on that understand- ing. The motion was carried unanimously.

ST. PAUL'S CHURCH, WIDNES.

The annual tea party and concert was held in the Parochial Room on Monday evening. About 400 pei sons sat down to an excellent repast. Trays had been given by Mesdames linison, Edwards, Deacon, Wood, Fletcher, Arnell, Rowntree, Barker, F. Neil, Valentine, Price, Puryer, W. Palin, R. Potter, Brookes, Mitchell, Hunt and Laws; Rev. W. Hodgson, Messrs. S. Deacon, W. Borthwiek, Dr. MacLennan, Dr. Allen and '• W.W." After tea a concert was held and the proceedings were made also to serve as a welcome to the " new curate," the Rev. J. Adams, M.A., and to fittingly recognise the removal of Mr. Fred Neil to Southport, and therefore his severance with St. Paul's. Mr. H. Wade Deacon, J.P., C.C., presided and in the course of a speech, dwelt upon the harmony that had prevailed amongst the various sects in the Royal Marri- age Celebration and the Distress Fund. He also alluded in eulogistic terms to the personal characteristics of Mr, Neil and the good work he had accomplished in the church and in connection with the Town Council and Free Library. Speeches to the same effect weft deliv- ered by the Rev. G. H. Garratt, Rev. W. Hodgson, Mr. J. C. Crawford and Mr. D. J. Brookes, and then the Chairman handed to Mr. Neil a handsome illuminated address, which was embellished by the Coat of Arms of the borough and by a photograph of St. Paul's Church. The text of the address was as follows : "To Frederick Neil. Esq. Dear Sir,—The loss of so es- teemed and valued a friend of the parish of Widnes as yourself affords us opportunity, although much to be re- gretted, of testifying to the genuine feelings of regard and gratitude which all in Widnes bear to your family and yourself. Your lifelong connection with our church, Your ever ready and willing help in all that was con- aucive to the best interests, will not easily pass from our minds and memories. In particular your close connec- tion with the church, and afterwards as warden for seven years, was marked by increasing assiduity and un- failing atte•aticn to duty, and has left among us a per- manent proof of self-denying effort and a generous heart. Such services as yours bring their own reward, and such as it is not in our power to bestow ; but we trust that you will receive this slight token of our friendship and gratitude as an earnest of our good wishes for the future welfare of your family and yourself, and cur heartfelt prayer that God's blessing may attend you through all your future life. Dated this day, Ist Jan., 1394. Signed, on behalf of the parish of Widnes, W. Hodgson, vicar; D. J. Brookes and W. Dwerrybouce, churchwardens."

In acknowledging the gift Mr. Neil said he had done nothing to deserve such a recognition, he had simply done his duty. Although he had left Widnes he was not going to be buried, and if ha could serve them at any future time he should be glad to do it. He was retain- ing the position of treasurer of the Parochial Rooms and of the Day Schools.—The Rev. Mr. Adams received a most cordial welcome.

MIRACLF.

INTERVIEWING UP TO DATE.

A good piece of work has been done by the" Glasgow Mail," a leading Scottish bewspaper. The report of a " miracle" said to have occurred in an outlying place having created censitierable local sensation, a "Glasgow Mail " reporter was sent to the spot to thorouehly in- vestigate the matter at first hand. His impressions have leeen pub!ished at great length in a lively article. The first view of the new local celebrity, one Peter Ross, of Westfield, near Elgin, seems to have confirmed the reports eft hand. This man had been paralysed, horribly tormented by pains in the back, and so brpught down by incurable disease. locomotor ataxy,t hat he hail to be lifted in and out of a vehicle in order even to travel even a short distance. But when the interview& called, he stepped briskly into the room.and a neighbour reported having seen him on a bicycle! This therefore was a genuine miracle to all appearances, and a personal interview with Mr. Ross and his Mother, so far from explaining it away, made it appear all the more wonder- ful.

" About three years ago," said Mr. Ross, "I was working in Greenbrae Quarries at Hopeman, when I got a jerk. I felt a sudden pain in the small of my back ;as time went on this increased, and a fortnight later I saw a doctor, who advised me to go into the hospital at Elgin, whither I went, and from which I was discharged about a month later. But I had scarcely started when the pains returned, and I was worse than ever. I tried mineral waters at Strathpeffer, but this treatment was of no use. My legs wer.. now beginning to fail me, and I staggered like a drunken man." On the advise of a medical man in Forres,he went into Aberdeen Hospital, being lifted into and out of the con- veyance which took him there. By this time he could not stand alone. After two months traatinent.the dread word " incurable" filled the poor fellow with an intense yearning for home. So he was carried down the hos- pital stairs, lifted into a carriage, driven home, lifted out, and carried again into his own house to die. Six months of lingering agony followed. "No power in my legs," said the man, pathetically, in answer to the in- terviewer's queries : " a pain indescribable across my brow and down my nose ; a sickness more nauseous than I can tell ; a pain in my back serer than I can describe. I got out of bed and triA to walk from one chair to an- other, but fell like a child who has not learned to walk!' What wonder that the poor fellow longed for death to release him from his sufferings? But three months ago the miraculeus relief came. AN Scotland was talking of D3nald Turner, the Lochelpe head man, who had been cured of f aralysi, almost as bad as Mr. Ross', by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. Ross determined to test the effect of the same remedy. "Let inc again impress upon 3 ou," lie said to the "Glasgow Mail" reporter, "that before I got the Pills, death would have been a relief. Weil, I got a box of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills, and before I had taken half- a-dozen I felt myself getting batter. By the time the box was finished there was a still further improvement, the pain was passing away from my back, and the power was returning to my limbs. I had spent pounds on medicine. But when I had taken Dr. Williams' Pink Pills I became a new man. I've a rare appetite; the sickness, the racking headache, and pain in the back have left me. I .3n walk to and from Elgin, which is nine miles. lam able to take jobs of work, and, though the electors told me I should never be better, I am now hale and hearty."

Such a remarkable story appeared to demand confir- mation, and the canny Scotch reporter set out to get it. Mrs. Sutherland, a neighbour of Ross, fully confirmed every word of his story. Had never expected to see him back from Aberdeen Infirmary, when he was lifted into the carriage to go there. Another neighbour, Mrs. Les- lie, another highly respectable witness, said there was DO possible doubt that Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Faa9. People had saved Ross's life. Nevar expected to see him rise from his sick bed, but could see for herself that he was now as well as ever.

The reporter was, therefore, able to conirm by indis- putable evidence, all that had been said of this remark- able case, which is not, by many hundreds, the first of the cures wrought by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People, in cases of rheumatism, neuralgia, paralysis, locomotor ataxy, St. Vitus'dance, nervous headache and prostration ; diseases of the blood, such as scrofula, chronic erysipelas, &c. THE PROPOSED MUSIC HALL FOR WIDNES.

At the Widnes Petty Sessions on Thursday, before Mr. T. Sutton Timrnis and Councillor Gossage, George Mellon, Market-street, applied for a music- hall license for a wooden structure in Ditton-road, used till recently as a circus. Mr. H. L. Riley, salicitor,St.Helens,applied in support of the application. Mr.J.W.Knowles,on behalf of the proprietors of the Alex- andra Theatre, Widnes, opposed the application. Mr. 'l'. Sutton Timmis said he might say he had inspected the place as also had the superintendent of police and the borbligh surveyor. The surveyor said he had not made his report yet,and if the application was proceeded with, the bench, under the circumstances, would not feel dis- posed to grant it. fie (Mr. rimmis) recommended that the application should be adjourned till the report of Mr. Sinclair (the surveyor) was made. Before the license could be granted a hydrant would have to be fixed, the sanitary arrangements would have to receive attention, and the meana of egress and ingress, which were quite inadequate, would have to be improved. Mr. Riley sub- mitted that by the provisions ot the public health amendment act of 1890 all that the bench were concerned with were the purposes of the proposed entertainment and the fitness of the person proposed to be licensed for giving that entertainment. The Chairman admitted Mr. Riley's definition of the law, and was unwilling to put the applicant to 'any unnecesFary expense, but the applicant had no one but himself to blame. A fortnight ago the applicant went before the court without any plans, without any evidence, and asked for a license. The place was utterly ußfit for such a licenseand he kthe chairman) thought the best thing would be to put the place in a proper condition and then apply fpr a license. The applica'ion was adjourned till Februarl 8.

" BETTY AND BILLY."—A CHILD'S TALE OF THE SHIP, CANAL BY MRS..H.N. HOUGHTON, WARRINGTON.—Just now when there is ss, much interest taken in the Ship Canal. "Betty and hilly" ought to be well appreciated and wel- comed by all ~f Us. It is suggestively written, and cleverly illustrated with full page coloured drawings and a number if smaller sketches: two of which we recog- nize as the Barley Mow in the Market Place, and the Manchester Town Hall. In one of the pictures we were surprised and somewhat amused to see gulls flying about the Ship Canal, but when we reached Pomona Dock,,, Salford, on New Year's DAy, we were still more sur- prised to Re the gulls really there before us—about a dozen of them. The" tale" is rery sketchy and imagin- ary—a sort of fairy tale founded on fact—sure to please the little folk& John Heywood of Manchester, is the publisher, sod Messrs. Chorlton and Knowles, Man- chester, are the printers of the book.

lifittstrai 1115U:1'11mm-5.

IT is not difficult to understand Compromise. and to sympathise with the dis- appointment that has bee n caused to some of the Radical members cif Par- liament by the compromise the Government

has made with the Opposition over the remaining stages of the Parish Councils Bill. It is indeed questionable whether it was advisable, even as a matter of expdiency, for the Government to en-

tort am any suggestions of compromise under the

circumstances. The Tories have all along ob- structed this Bill deliberately and systematically. They have been regularly beaten in fair argu-

ment and on the legitimate battle ground of the division lohbia. But, in order to gain their ends, they have unblustingly resorted to obstruc- tion of the most flagrant kind. The forms of Parliament have bee). wrenched and abused by these self-styled constitutional purists to an ex-

tent that has never been witnessed before. Now, it is to be feared, that the obstructionists will lay the flattering unction to their souls that they

have secured by unfair tactics what they could not obtain by the fair and open methods of Parlia-

.ment. They will say that obstruction has been justified by succe.ss, and this may mean increased

trouble in the immediate future. There is, o.

course, a great deal to be said in favour of the

action of the Government. The Parish Councils Bill contains much useful reform which is un-

affected by the concessions made to the reaction-

cry forces in Parliament. A lot of time and not a little sacrifice on the part of Ministers and their supporters have been expended in carrying the Bill as far as it has gone, and to have seen it wrecked after all by unscrupulous opponents would have been a grievous disappointment. The value of the compromise will greatly depen pen the action of the House of Lords. The q tion is— ere tile majority of the Peers, who are at the mere beck and call of the leaders of the Tory party, bound by the agreement as well as the Tory minority in the House of Commons? In the or- dinary course of things this question would be answered with an unhesitating affirmative, but in the present instance we are assured by Tory organs in the Press that the Peers are in no way restricted in their actions, by the 2. greement. If that be so, the compromise gives no security for the ultimate passing of the Bill as it now stands. The measure will pass from the Commons in a less democratic form than it was originally planned, but it will be handed over for. dissection 'ta Chamber which is inplacably hostile to:every demo- cratic principle in it. If the Lords are stilt to have a free hand,it is to be feared that the Bill will be mutilated almost out of recognition.

The conditions which the Tories of the House of Commons have thought fit to impose are a. fine exposure of the claims oi that party to be the "friends of the people." There could, in fact,. be

no clearer display oi the real objects-for which the party fights. In regard to,Boprds of Guardians, for instance, they have opposed tooth and:nail the

principle ef popular election,, and one of the conditions they have exacted practioally amounts to the retention of the ex-qpio, element on these bedies. The as altered to, suit Tory tastes, now provides. that four members of Boards of , Guardians may be elected': by each , Board without their names being submitted to the ratepayers. Every one-has seen how ihis sort of thing works out in practice. Men who, would ,never be elected by a popular vote of r4aepayers, .or who disdain to submit themselvea to that popular vote,are by a side wind placed in positions in which they control the expenditure of public , money and have great influence- in moulaing the policy of public bodies. We had thought that the distrust of the voters which is implied in these co-optative appointments had, been discarded by all polfticians in these days,.but it ia- clear that the Tories cling to it with as much tenacity as ever they did. Thsn,as to allotments,the reaction- ary spirit of Toryism has displayed itself in a fashion which is in the highest degree harmful not only to the agricultural labourers-but to) the whole of the country. A change in the, Bill that has been insisted upon is that out of the- four acres which a labourer may have from the Parish Coun- cil only one acre can be used for tillage, the other three to be laid down to grass. There is a fa- tuousness in this condition, ancl a positive disre- gard for the welfare of the country as a whole that is really astounding. It means, a direct prohibi- tion upon that increase 'of-the, annual wealth of the country which it ought to be almost the first aim of every. statesman to encourage. At a time when there, is , a cry from. every direction that English land is not producing what it is capable of producing„ here we have Toaiea who call them- selves sta.tesinen insisting that out of every four acres whic,ii an iudustrious agricultural labourer may succee(l in hiring,. three acres shall . be- per- manently kept out of tillage. In other words, if the Tories have their- way, three-fourths of the land which may be allotted under the new Biil will IA) reduced as- nearly as possible to the. bare prairie value. Land in tillage, and especially land. in the hands oi industrious men who culti- vate, with the spade,. is infinitely more productive thaia land in grass ;. therefore, say the Tories, three out of four-acres must be in grass. Snob a senseless, and i some respects almost criminal, locking-up of the wealth-producing powers of the soil should he reprobated in every part of the country.

cated Spanish workman, who has cherished Anar- chist principles from Ma youth upwards, Like all the rest of the bomb-throwers he is a hopeless fanatic. He expresses no contrition for his deed, or for the misery and suffering he has caused. The only matter that causes him any regret is that more people were not killed. He did his best to ensure a wholesale slaughter, but his scheme partly mis- carried. He threw two bombs, but only one of them exploded. He declares that he had no accem- plices,and that he fabrics ted the bombs according 'to the prescribed formula. Prescribed by whom it would be interesting to know. It is said that the people of Barclona breathe more freely now. that the real perpetrator of the atrocious crime is in safe keeping, and that is not surprising. FRANCII, for that is the name of the Anarchist, had evidently made up his mind to wage a ruthless war , against Society, and had he been left at liberty he would probably have been heard of again. In France the proceedings against Valliant who threw the bomb in the Chamber of Deputies are being pressed forward without much regard to the usual legal forms. This is regret- able, for as the Paris correspondent of the "Daily News" says," Nobody can say what form of government may or may not arise in rrance. The precedents that are being now made will be invaluable to a third em pire, or any other gov- ernment that would have to streggle for existence against the forms now in use and the •ideas they represent." It is said that the shoemaking trade furnishes the greatest number of Anarchists, and furthermore, that in most of the houses that have been searched the " blackest misery" prevailed. Anarchism, it s,voulel almost seem, is only another phase of the great unemployed question, for, t4iya the correspond° nt quoted above in reference to some of the Parisian Anarchists s " Enforced idle- ness turned them into dreamers,. and hunger and other effects of utter poverty made them indiffer- ent to what risks they run."

MR. AsQuiTH matie an inrportant

Dangerous Trades.

announeembrit in Parliament on

Thursday, in answer to Me. BURNS. It was to the effect that ho intended next session to propose legislation which would give the,, Secretary of State power to,deal with unreasonably long hours in the alkali and some other dangerous trades. Legislation; of the character referred to by the Homo Secretary will, we believe, be fraught with the greatest benefits to workpeople employed in the trades referred to, and we do not see that the employers will have ' much to complain of, as they will have equal treat- ment meted out to them. 3n the alkali trade, the large firm of Messrs. BRD.NNER, MOND; and CO have voluntarily set an excellent example in shortening the hours of their workmen, and, it utaY be added, with the most encouraging results. They, in 1890, at the request of; the men], reduced the daily shift from twelve to eight hours, and at the same time they increased the piece-work rates. After what may _ be called short period of transition, during which the re" suits were doubtful, they found themselves in a position to report to the Home Office that, "the cost of wages paid per ton of alkali is now no more than it • was four years ago, in spite of the fact that the men employed on shift work are paid the same wages for eight hours as they were formerly paid for twelve. Ono result of the creditable " new departure taken by the famous Northwich firm was, a; reduc- tion in the percentage of the men who, had to be attended by the doctor from 10.12 in 1889; to 54 in 1893. In dealing with the long hours which still prevail in sonic centres of the alkali traderthe HOME SECRETARY will have his hands strength- ' ened by the results of the experiment which '• Messrs. BRUNNER NOND and Co. teak the risk of ' carrying out.

Thi • dinner, as usual, consisted of substantial roast. bee, and plum pudding, veal and rabbit pie,. c. 0, usual allowance of half a pint of beer was made t.. those who liked it, whilst to teetotalers othe' drinks were supplied,axid after dinner all were tioP; plied with tobacco and pipes. TRADE.

The notices for an increase of wages given by several sections of the building trade in St. Helens and counter-notices for a reduction of wages given by the masters have caused a good deal of uneasi- ness and a very unsettled and unstable feeling. The dispute will be referred to a board of arbitration to consist of six masters and six representatives of the men, and if a settlement cannot be. arrived at an umpire may be appointed. The board will meet before the expiration of the six months' notices, which terminate in May.

A. PECULIAR INCIDENT AT WOOLSTON. On Saturday, a respectably dressed woman, named Clara Smith, and hailing from Manchester, called at a house in Woolston for a drink of water. This was supplied her, and on her turning to leave the yard a dog which was chained near the door, sprang at her and bit her severely through the left hand. Some words passed between her and the woman at the house, and it appears that Smith lost her temper and smashed some of the windows. A. policeman was called, and. she was taken -co the Padgate Police Station. The injuries .to her hand Were so seveie that Sergeant Barnes deemed it ad- visable to have her immediately attended to, and she was accordingly released and sent to ‘Var- rington. DISTRIBUTION OF TOYS AND C/lILDRBN'IS ETn

TAINMENT AT THE WORKHOUSE

On FriK.;;Jternoon last week, a very eniovnble time was spent in the schoolroom at the Work- house, the oceasion being the annual Christmas gift of toys to all children in the house, whose ages vary from babies of a few days old to boys and girls of 11 and 12 years. The toys were bought with subscriptions given by the ',entry and trades- people in Warrington and neighbourhood. This year an innovation was made in the programme by an entertainment given by the children, who had been taught for some weeks by Miss Ward. The pro- gramme was only a short one, but the carrying out of it gave great pleasure to the little performers as well as the Guardians present. It consisted of an action song, called " Welcome," followed by musical drill, and a recitation entitled "Santa Claus." Then toys were given out with fruit, &c., to all the chit- (l,-en who were made very happy by their poses- sions. The Chairman, Mr. George Ratcliffe,in a few words expressed his pleasure with the entertain- ment and wished Miss Ward success in her work. The Guardians present were :—Messrs. A. J. Jolley, A. Jolley, W. J. Shaw, W. Houghton, Geo. Clarke, Joseph Dobson, J. Turton, E. Auklawl, James Bill, the Master and Matron (Mr. and Mrs. Ward), and other officials of the house with the inmates. FACILITIES FOR SAVINGS BANK DEPOSITORS. The Savings Bank Bill of Session 1893, having re- ceived the Royal assent, has become law, and de- positors in Savings Banks may now deposit up to £5O in one year, and may deposit for purchase of Government, stack up to £2OO in one year. Na doubt, many of the large number of depositors (over 10,000 in our local Savings Bank, in Buttermarket- ' street,will be pleased to hear of the above mentioned increased limits of deposit, and will take advantage of the new provisions of the new act, of Parlia- ment. . BLANKET CHARITY." A useful blanket charity called the Eilanket Charity,"has made its first annual distribu- tion ot good warm blankets, at. the rooms occupied by the Charity Investigat ion Society, Xing-street, amongst a goodly number ol very poor, clean-look- ing, and well-known respectable persons, chiefly women, some of whom had evidently seen happier days. From inquiries made, it appears that the late Mr. Alderman Shaw Thewlis,of Latchford House,who died a few years ago, amongst other charitable be- quests left a sum ot £5OO to be invested through the Charity Commissioners in Government securi- ties, the interest of which should be paid over to trustees under his will,after the death of his widow , (who died 18 months ago) and that they should on the fifth day of November in each year,the anniver- • sary of his birthday, purchase with the said inte- rest, a quantity of good blankets and distribute theta amongst the moat deserving and necessitous poor they could find, who-would be most likely to use them as was intended by the late Alderman. Consequently, on Tuesday morning, the late Alder- man Thewlia's trustees (Alderman Harrison and Mrs. Elijah Harris) met a number of poor women at the above roams, who had been strongly recommended as recipients of the blankets. and after addressing a few kindly words and good wishes for the new year, proceeded to distribute the blankets, much to the , delight of the poor people, who expressed their grateful thanks for the seasonable gifts.

The Chairman E-,ait-i--us-being the first meeting in the Niew Year he wished them all a very happy and pros- perous one.

' HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. The Medical Officer reported that the number of deaths registered during December amounted to 47; five Oh these were inquest cases and of the remaining 42 twelve were due to bronchitis, tWo to whooping cough, five to pneumonia, six to premature birth, three to meseuterie disease, three to heart disease, two to enteric fever, one each to senile decay, dropsy, inflammation Of the heart, influenza, diabetes, convulsions, phthisis, and apoplexy. These deaths showed an average death rate of 28 2 and exclusive of the inquest cases of 25.2. There were 16 deaths amongst infants under 12 months. —The Surveyor said in the corresponding month of 1892 there were 40 deaths.

THE CANAL BOATS ACTS. The Markets Committee submitted the annual report, mitten by the Secretary, with respeet to the working •1 the Canal Bwat Acts of 1877 and 1884 and it was ordered to be sent to the Local Government Board. Me report stated that during the 'past year the Authority lial registered 21 boats, 19 of which were new boats, 1, re-registered because of structural alterations and one which had been registered with another authority mak- ing a total of 963 boats registered at Runcorn. The Inspector had reported 10 contraventions of the Acts and f the regulations thereunder. He had from time to time advised the Canal Boats Committee that all these defects had been remedied. 303 Inspections of boats had been made during the year and the Inspector foand that on board the boats visited were 493 men 123 women and 133 children. Four cases of sickness were notified under 1113 Infectious Diseases (Notification) Act 1889 as having occurred on board canal boats. The Committee were pleased to report that there had been a decrease in the :mintier of women and children on board the boats as compared with last year. The salary of the Ingpeetor is £2O per annum, with allowance of 2. per boat examined for registration.
