In the circular issued, there was this statement : It has been decided that sea going vessels arriving in Manchester during the year 1894 will not be charged any slop dues. Sea going vessels tred- ing in the Ship Canal up to Runcorn will cc-Jail-Me to pay the ship dues now levied ; vessels trading the Canal 1 from Runcorn to a. point • near Warrington will pay lower rats than Rumeen, while vessels trading to airy part of tire Canal between Warrington and Manchester will nit he charged any ship dues. He considered that was a manifast in- ) to tileir Runcorn traffic. (Hear, heer) and an imposition or chargas that ought net to he. It was really giving preference Lo port over port in their charges and it should nut he allowed. They ought t,) demand fair treatment in this raspec:. The Bill gave no power to the company to penalize Runcorn iu the way they were now doing in dues, whilst other portions were exempt ; and if they appealed to the right quartars he believed they would get redress to a manifest injustice. It was Only the other day a ship owner showed him a bill for dues a mountiog to £2l 151., which he said it they had to be paid would deter him from sending his steamers to Runcorn. Thus it was apparent that by the attempt to impose those dues it was an effort to force the traffic from Runcorn up the Canal, which meant a great diminution of the employment which had hitherto been provided in the town. It was manifest that the interests ot Runcorn were to be sacri- fieed, so that places further on might receive advantage. The Commissioners in every reasonable way had met the Canal Co., and the proof of this was the giving op of one lock, which meant a saving of £70,000 or £BO,OOO. Then again, they had agreed to lose their status as a port, to satisfy the ambition of Manchester. Notwith- standing this, they were being treated in the manner mentioned. He thought it behoved them to bestir themselves in that matter, and to look after their in- terests. He was pleased to know that those engaged in the shipping trade were bestirring themselves in the matter, and lie trusted that Board would give them every assistance. With regard to Runcorn being reduced to a " creek," no notiea was given to them until the Saturday before they had to meet the Treasury on the Monday. It had been already stated that they had all the privileges and advantages of a port conferred upon them. Under these circumstances he ' should like to know why they had lost the title of port It was simply to satisfy the ambition of a higher auth- ority. Were they justified in doing that, considering the treatment, they were receiving from them ? Perhaps it might nut be too late even vet to again approach the Treasury on the subject. The title was obtained by hard work many years ago, and it might not be teo late to reopen communications on the point. He believed that the Mancnester Ship Canal Co. were seeking to be freed from submitting their schedule of charges to the Board of Trade, and it was quite possible for Runcorn to be represented when their charges were formulated. It was their bounden duty to sea that their rights were obtained. The clauses were obtained at considera'ele expense, they having spent more than £1,200, and now the Canal Co. wanted to make these clauses void, ale considered that Runcorn had been hardly dealt with by the imposition of those charges—there had been no consideration shown to them as an old and ancient

port. Mr. Lightburn endorsed everything that Mr. Pritchard had said with reference to the dues. They had been very unfairly treated, and he should be sorry if no con- cession was made by the Canal Co. respecting them. They had treated the promotors of the undertaking in a friendly manner, and he should not like to see that spirit disturbed, but the publication of those charges did not teed in that direction. Mr. Risbrown thought it should go forth that the Board were keenly alive to the interests of the commu- nity. Tuey had approached a crisis in the history of their town, for in tin: near future Runcorn might be made or marred. They were - willing, therefore, as an authority, to assist any effort w hich would rive bene- ficial to the residents. The Chairman endorsed every 'mei d that had been said on the subject. The question was very important to the On He repeated the statement that that Board had treated the company in all their dealings with fair- ness, and he therefore felt that the present action was a great injustice to Runcorn. He felt sure from past experience that if the questions were fairly represented to the Canal Coy. they would reconsider the matter. The discussicn then ceased.

'MR /YARDS 411rSTION. ( The General Purposes Committee submitted a me- 1 morial to the Cheshire County Council for adeptiou with respect to the division of the distriet of Itencorn into ward. It set forth that the Board consisted of eight- teen members. At the time of the formation of it the number of assessments to the poor rate in the district was 1,867, the rateable value being £22,742, and the population, according t) the census of 1851, 8,683. The number of assessments at the present time is 4,315, the rateable value being -258,000,and the population,accord- ing to the census of 1891, 20,053 At the date on the formation of the urban district the inhabited area (ex• cept a small portion of the township) was almost alto- gether on the north side of the Bridgewater Canal and between such canal and the river Mersey. Since that time large districts, formerly devoted to agriculture, have been added to the inhabited area, both on the east,west, and south, and from the fact of its being the terminus of the Bridgewater Canal and the Trent and Mersey Navigation, and also its importance as contaieing large docks belonging to the Manchester Ship Canal Company, the growth of the town and district may be reasonably expected to continue. The election of members on the Board has been held under the commissioners Clauses Act, 1847, one-third of the members retiring each year, and the election taking place at one polling station. For many years the mode . of election as prescribed by the Act has been felt to be very unsatisfactory, and it has failed to elicit a full or even adequate expression of the opinion of the rate- payers, as not more than one-fourth have at any one , election exercised their franchise. The desire for a ' change has often been expressed in public meeting, but as an extension of local government was inevitable on: the passing ef the Local Government Act, 1888, it was deemed wise to defer taking active steps to secure an Improved method or system until the expected eNtensioß was within r.-.cr.',;U.11:; :listance, Tilt ,RlqwAi I furthPr Poirit eut that teAr:ll tin 110iaiite4Oit Q't laul' Cifts.`dl3llll On those of past loiN4l School Board and County ICouncil elections, the cost of an election will be from £lOO to £l3O, but the c/it in those ceses is spread over a Reritd of tt;reNl ream: in the case of an urban district then "Oil be An auntie( election of one-third of the mem- bers, so that this amount would probably be incurred' reach year if the district be undivided ieto wards; the experience of other urban districts, however, proves that seldom are more than one-third of the werds contested annually, and thus on the ground of expense, your memorialists respectfully submit that it is ex- tremely desirable that the division of the mean district into wards should he made in order ti meet new awl altered conditions. This opportunity having nova ar- rived by the introduction of the Local Government Bill, 1893, thA subject hae been discussed by the Board of Im- provement Commissioners after due notice, and the fol- lowing resolution passed thereon :—"That this Board be- ing of opinion that the division of the Runcorn Urban Sanitary District into wards would greatly facilitate the recording of votes in Parliamentary and local elections, and would he further itstrutuental ia securing increased efficiency in the conducting of local affairs, hereby instructs the Parliamentary and General Purposes Committee. with Mr. Ilandley's name added, to prepare and present to the next meeting of the Board a draft application to the Cheshire County Council to exercise the powers con- ferred by section 57 of the Local Government Act of 1888, and institute an inquiry as to the advisability of such division,and to make such order thereon as may be deemed fit—such order not to come into force until the passing of the District Councils Bill." Mr. Pritchard said when the Bill was being promoted in Parliament, the Board thought it wise, considering that it was proposed to cut off their free uninterrupted communication with the river, that they should ask for the free use of the Canal so that the traffic to Runcorn might have the facilities it had hitherto had. The Board engaged counsel and appeared in Committee of the House of Lords to sustain that contention, but before the Bill came to the House of Commons they made ar- rangements with the promoters far halt dues to Runcorn. That was the greatest concession that had been given to any Authority up to that time upon this section. But the Ellesmere Port people continued their opposition with considerable vigour on to the close, and they obtained the free use of the Canal up to 400 tons. At the time the exemption clause was given to the .Ceminissioners the promoters made a statement to the effect that any concession made to any other Authority upoa this section that Runcorn should be entitled to the same advantage. Well they had asked for the same concession in the cor- respondence that had taken place,and they had received a reply that the directors did not consider Runcorn was entitled to benefit under the exemption clause. SOME YANKEE YARNS.

BEING ACTUAL EXPERIENCES.

gY LUKE BLUNT.

We were sitting together, chatting about things in geqieral, when the conversation turned upon foreign experiences. Both Willotson and Benz ii had travelled America,and were soon interchanging opinions and experiences. The drink question and tile public morals of the great c4ntinenr were under debate. Both parties were agrect! that taken gen- erally, there was more vice of all sorts in the large cities in Amex ice, than in cities of the same size in England. " English people "said Willotson" ore very much mislead on this subject. 1 haTe 14eea letters and articles by the dozen in English journals—includ- ing Warrini,Tton.papers—in which the writers vox that they have spent days without seeing a drunken' person in American cities. It is simple nonsense. I have been in New York, in Chicago. in New Or- leans,and in Boston, and I have seen in those places more drinking, gambling and general vice than in' any four large cities in the United Kingdom. The people who come with these flattering reports are evidently persons who remain pretty closely in one sphere of society,—in the religious sects. I can as- sure you " concluded Willotson sinding" whisky drinkers are abnormally developed—or their drink- ing propenseties are, in America."

"That reminds me of a very remarkable experience that happened me in a prohibition State " put in Benzon. " 1 was invited by a Puritan family to a coming of age party. Besides the fonr sons and two parents there were several friends present. Everything was carried out in a strictly Puritanical spirit—there was not even a smell of intoxicating liquors to be discovered. As the night wore on, however, one of the sons approached me and asked if I would like to take a mouthful of fresb air on the verandah. We strolled out together end pre- sently my companion began to fumble about his breast pocket and said, in a slightly embarrassed way: "Sort of had an idea Benzon that maybe soft' drinks airnt al-tigetner in your line." " Ne ! Not entirely so."

" Wall " nervooly " have a ball' (a drink) out of this 'en. It's whisky sours." Ile handed me the flask and went on hurriedly to explain. "It's this way. The old people and brothers ere all down on liquor. But I like to have jest a drop by as medi- cine—you follow—jest fer emergencies; sort of foolish to push principles too far. So I keep this bottle unknown to them; 'cause it would sort of hurt their feelings. You follow." We quailed and then returned indoors. In about half-an-hour the father, a long-faced, smileless old Puritan came,and took me by the arm, and said : " In the midst of our tribulations we find solace Mr. Benzon. Yo'r my solace for this evening, for I'm going to leadern the frivolity of this evening by a sober chat with yo' on this troubled question of the tariff. Come right along o' me to my room Mr. Benzon."

Atter a few minutes discussion on the tariff the old man said ; " To aint a prohibition man Benzon. o! No more am I. But the family air—they an bound with invulnerable bonds to the question of temperance, Mr. Benzon, and it would snook their unsophisticated hearts if the old man broke the . unanimity of the family. So I keep a little flask of sours away up here, and I have a plug now and then, and t key aint any wiser. Don't be afraid on it Mr. Benzon—it aint forty rod." Well a short time afterwards a younger brother asked me on to the verandah. Precisely the same ; thing hsppeued as with the other brother. I was beginning to get frisky, and can correctly describe myself a 9 being in good spirits. By and by, the ol,:t tla.mtn bobl,Aing up. She sala I "I was just saying to Mrs. Harper how yo' resemble my young brother—Jim, he died o' small pox, pc; goad soul—To' beaver so much like him. To' aint seen his photo! No! Come right along with me, then." She grew pathetic ever that photo, and fell to weeping a trifle. Presently she said " Alt me ! What a soft, old soul I be, Mr. Benzon. The least thine puts me out, and I don't know as how I should get thro' if it wern't for a nip o' whisky. Oid Ben nor the boys don't know it—l sometimes wish they did—but oh ! what treuM they think !" This whisky was weaker than the other, but it made me begin to feel suspicious about myself. I would probably have pulled through, however, had it not been that still another brother, under the pretence of showing me the melon patch by moon- light, took me out and told me he was the only black sheep in the family—the oaly one given to secret drinking. Though I began, after this last draught, to distrust everything I did or said, I should still have tried to brave matters out had I not, upon returning,seen the old man endeavouring to catch my eye again. I knew another round of confidences would be fatal to my sense of sedate- ness2-so I left."

" Well," said Willotson, "speaking about whisky sours reminds me of a curious meeting I had with an old Western saloon-keeper. It was in a small place in the State of Montana. We were passing ihrough on a prospecting tour. We stayed at the., only saloon. There were three of us. The sole refreshment to be obtained was whisky. So two of us ordered,' whisky straights' (unmixed whisky). the other ordered a' whisky sour.' !Inv!!e whikk- ~,trA.;--1-t." drawled, tne 1241 i '"4 • .__,per,Lt " said a 'whisky sour`." "Yew have it • straight'." " I'll have what I ordered."

'` Yew're to have a ' whisky straight,'" thundered the man, pulling out a pistol and point- ing it at our friend who quietly submitted. When this was over the saloon-keeper grew quite friendly, and hospitable, awl in a confidential outburst he admitted that When yew got in yewr lett I had to hunt grass, part. There was only one trick as I knew to play again that whisky sour.' But its all fixed now, so pur, it thar, pard "(offering his liauo). This metaphorical speech meant that the saloon-keeper did not know how to mix a whisky sour.' and to his way of looking at the matter it was more honourable for him to shoot our friend, than to admit his own ignorance.

" Well, only a month after that I entered, in the same State, one of the old-fashioned prayer meet- ing houses. And I saw quite as extraordinary an incident there. The congregation consisted chiefly of pious farmers and a few religiously inclined miners. It was a very pious, and sombre congrega- tion—such a thing as the shadow of a smile never once disturbed the imperturbable aspect. of deep devotion which brooded on every face. It was a true type of a settlement congregation. Towards the close of the service the minister, as is the cus- tom, asked for some brothers to please take round the boxes.' I noticed a young man, who looked like a broken-down clerk from an Eastern State, get up and pass round the box near the top end of the building. Presently, the voice of the minister broke the silence by solemnly uttering : " If that young man leaveth the Lord's House with that box, he will surely be damned." "And l'il be denied if he hasn't," gravely remarked a sallow-faced farmer, to my right, who quietly followed the strange young man out. The reports of two pistol shots in the road were heard audibly from the meeting house, and in a few moments the sallow-faced farmer, quite unruffled,tip-toed gently to the rostrum and there deposited the stolen box. I Then he quietly returned to his seat."'

The conversation next turned upon the different manner in which the Southern States people take to improvements which are generally eagerly adopte-d by the people in the Eaetern States. "In New Orleans," said Willotson, "the inhabitants as a rule take very badly to innovations in the way of improvements. When I was there, not long ago, the first electric tram had been introduced. The old cars had been drawn formerly by a single mule, and the people were very loth to forsake them for the improvement. The only person, indeed, who appeared to appreciate the new car was a negro,who acted as servant to me. Respecting these cars he one day said : "Golly, Marser, white man is a gemmen, en dere aint no doubt on de marten" "Indeed Jake. Whets he done now ?" -

" What he done ! Giong Mar, yo' foolin'Jakee. Ee-ee-e !" • " tio ! what is it." "Why white man—why, fus he come an' free de , nigger, an' den Limner, an' den he come an' free de mule. Golly! Elecktrick car am tine." P.S.—As the facts contained in my contributions lire generally beyond all question, so far as veracity goes, I would point out that while believing the above 'yarns 'to be quite true, I do net stake my reputation upon their authenticity. I value very much my reputasion for veracity, for, to quote :t Well-known author, truth oczes from me like otter of roses oozes from the otter.

THINGS HEARD AND SUN.

AT WARRINGTON.

The Wallington" J.P.'s " where conspicuous )jt their absence at the Police Court, on Monday mon- ing. Mr. T. Birchall was the only magistrate wlo made it his business to attend at the appointed time. Several important cases were awaiting to ln heard, and they necessitated the attendance of tw) magistrates, it was 'New Year's Day,and one would have thought that the magistrates would have bea anxious to make a good beginning. But no! thre was the informal opening of the Shin Canal to tempt them into unheeding the good old maxim " Business before pleasure," and, some at least yielded to the temptation. More than a score Rf persons ware present in court, and they were kept silently looking at each other whilst the police were telephoning and " hunting up" another magistrate. At last they succeeded with Mr. John Burgess al- though it was rot the appointed day for that gentleman, and at ten minutes to eleven the busi- ness of the court was proceeded with.

The members of the Corporation thoroughly enjoyed their trip along the Ship Canal to Manches- ter on Monday—evecially one member. I refer to Councillor Smethurst. The worthy councillor must have a rare pair of lungs, for he scarcely ever gave them a rest from the time of leaving Worrington to the journeys end. He led the cheering all the way. A man of his temperament is just the man for an occasion like the opening of the canal, for he keeps everybody alive, but by the by, what did he do with his tall hat between Warrington and Manches- ter?

The Helvetia made a good show along the course, end Mr.Sionks has every reason to be proud of her, for she beat every vessel which entered into com- petition with her, even beating the America, one of the largest of the Liverpool tug boats. There was one thing which I noticed was conspicuous by its absence in the procession, and which would other- wise have helped to enliven the proceedings. I refer to the absence of brass bands. A number of brass hands playing away at" Sailing, sailing, over the Ship Canal," would have been very appropriate to the occasion.

Warrington will soon be accustomed to hearing the sounds which seafaring vessels make. The noise of the syrens of the steamers when passing Moore and Thelwall can be heard quite distinctly in the. Bank Quay district. At first, they considerably surprised the inhabitants of that locality. They could not imagine for the life ot them what it was that was making such noises, and when told that it was the Ship Canal steamers, they were rather in- credulous. However, since the terrible noise made at Latchford locks last Monday, just before the procession commenced, all their incredulity has disappeared.

The Rev. Peter Mackenzie appeared at Bold-st., Wesleyan Chapel on Sunday and Monday, after about three years absence. lie should have given a lecture last year, but the smallpox epidemic frightened him away, as it did other lecturers. His comicality shows no signs of diminution, and roars of laughter were very frequent at both the sermons and lecture. The word Reverend before his name does not seem to be very appropriate, for he does not appear to have the " bump " of reverence to a very great degree. I have heard a tale about. Mr Mackenzie, for the accuracy of which Ido not vouch, but still, it is not at all improbable. In his college days, he would very frequently engage in outdoor service:?, at times not altogether approved of by the authorities of the college. Onone occa- sion he was holding one of these sorvic:es, when a ! brother student was 5e t to fetch him back. Seeing ttlt •-•.tudent coming, and gue,ising his errand, Mr. Mackenzie &terminal to get the better of him. With great solemnity, he called out " And now Mr.-- (referring to the student), will engage in prayer?" Needless to say, the student, did not get an immedi- ate chance of bringing back the the truant to the college. Mr. Mackenzie i& very popular all over the country, and, in regard to his lectures, is gener- ally' booked" for a place about two years before- hand.

ANNUAL MEETING.

The seventh annual meeting of the above building society was held in the Legii-street schoolroom on Thureday night. He had been looking through the reports of the society since its commencement, and had made a few notes which would be of interest to the members present.. He found that in the first year, their income was £ll3 es. 5d., and their outgo £l3O 9seleav- ing something like £l7 deficit, which was accounted for by formation expenses. Something over £6O had been gained, after all wore•e • expenses 'had been paid. (Applause.) BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

T m Errs.

At the Warrington B,rough Court on Monday, three petty theft cases were heard. Sarah James of Chester- road, Wilderspool was charged with stealing from tha shop of Edgar V. Morton, St. James'-street, a woman's shawl, value 4s. 61. Prisoner pleaded guilty. Joseph Ashton, assistant at Mr. Morton's shop, deposed that the prisoner came into the shop to pawn a shawl, and on going out she was seen to take the shawl prcducesl from the doorway. Information was given to the police and P.C. Bell found the prisoner in the lower, Bridge-street, pawning the shawl. She was under the influence of drink at the time. She was fined too costs, 19s.— James Hawkins and Alfonso Cullen were charged with stealing from the licensed premises of the Victoria Vaults on Saturday night, three lbs of beef, the property of the landlord. Peter Rafter. The evidence showed that the prisoner Hawkins was met by the landlaiy in the lobby with the beer under his coat. The beef was taken from the prispner. and it was found to be the property of the landlord. Thera was no evidence against Cullen, and he was discharged, but Hawkins was tined the costs, 19s.— Thomas Callighan was charged with stealing from the licensed premises of the Bridge Inn, about 7 a.m. on Sunday morning, a jar containing half a gallon of ruin, value 10s., the pr verty of Joseph Ashton. Sergeant Cheney said on looking inside the common lodging house at 23, Bank-street, about 7.30 on Sunday morning he saw the prisoner and another man drinking rum from the bottle produced. The prisoner's answers 'not being satisfactory as to how he became in possession of the rum, he was locked up. He soon afterwards admited taking it from the Bridge Inn. He had gone down the cellar early on Sunday morning, and filled the bottle from a cask. The magistrates fined the prisoner ss. including costs, considering that Mr. Ashton must knave left his souse in a very unguarded position ; and they hoped he Would be more careful in future. THEFTS FROld WARRINGTON MARKET.

She was scen to put some wool in her shawl, and aft Twards in her bas- ket. She was also seen by Detective Monaghan and Alfred Peacock to take a pair of stockings -and a pair of gloves from Mr. F. Littlewcod's stall. At Mr. Peacock's stall she purchased a child's pinafore for which she paid Bd. and whilst the parcel was being made up, Mr. Pea- cock saw her take three other pinafores from his stall. At the Warrington Borough Court on Friday, John Hollinc,worth, the "Roan Cow," St. James-street,Oswald Cook, Boon-street, Manchester, Thomas Shepherd, 20, New-road, Daniel Dag,nall, B,School-street,Alfked i atli or 18, Sparling-street, James Broadhurst, 20, New-road, Alfred Buckley, 24, Janaes-street, Henry Green, 19, New- road, Thomas Cook, 6 Sparrling-street,Thomas Caldwell, off Knutsford-road, James Taylor, 227, Mersey-view, Joseph Henry Taylor, 251, Mersey-view, and Frederick Sharpies, 201, Mersey-view. were each charged that on November 26, they unlawfully trespassed upon the London and North Western Railway, thereby expos- ing themselves to risk of danger, contrary ts, section 41 of the London and North Western Additional Powers Act, of 1883. Turner went to them and took their names and addresses. There was no crossing at the place where the defendants went over the railway. There were notice boards warning trespassers put up previous to the date of the present charge.—Cross

examined—Thera was a beaten track leading tO the metals on each side of the railway, but that was caused by poachers.—P.C. Turner gave corroborative evidence. —Mr. Browne said that his instructions were that no notice boards had been put up until the beginning of December. The day being Sunday, he contended that ' they were not in danger of their lives owiig to ' the fsw trains on that day .—Th9 magistrates considered that the charge was not a serious one, and fined each defendant 2s. 63. including costs.

A SERIOUS CHARGIC—James Kelly, was charged with being drunk and disorderly in Turner-st., at 6.45 on Saturday the 30th ult.,and further charged with asaault- ing P.C. Evans whilst in the execution of his duty, and also with assaulting a young woman named Alice Hough- ton at the same time and place. A remand was asked for until Saturday next. P.C. Evans said on Saturday night from information received, he went to Turner-st. He saw the defendant there drunk and disorderly. He had his shirt sleeves rolled up and was without coat or hat on, challenging to fight. The defendant was taken into austody, and on the way to the police station he was very rough. He kicked witness OH the ankle several times and butted him with his head, and it was with great difficulty he got him to the police station. Witness returned to 3, Turner-st., and there saw Alice Hough- ton, a young woman, lying unconscious. She had lost a large quantity of blood. Two witnesses were in court to prove that the prisoner had assaulted the woman. She was suffering from internal injuries, and ,would be un- able to appear for a week. He put them in the house and left them, but soon afterwards, he heard the woman screaming and moaning. He looked through the window and saw the woman on the floor and the male prisoner on the top of her striking her and threat- ening to cut her throat. They took the male prisoner into custody, but the female prisoner became very disorderly, and she was also taken into custody. NEWTON PETTY SESSIONS

SATURDAY.—Before Messrs. T. J. Gillespie (chairman), T. D. Grimke-Drayton, J. L. Wood, R. Stone, N. McCorquodale, and W. Mitchell. DRUNKENNESS.—MichaeI Kelly, Michael Costello, an d Michael Gill. all of Newton, were punished for cemmitt- ing this offence. BREACH OF TIIE PEACE.—Patrick Donellan,and Francis Riley were charged with committing a breach of the peace in Earle-st., Earlestown, by fighting on the 2nd ult. P.C. Burns proved the case and the defendants were bound over for three months. VIOLATIONS OF THE WEIOIIT3 AND MEASURES ACT.— Rebecca Locker was fined 10s. for having a pair of un- stamped scales in Earlestown, on the 6th ult.—Joseph Taylor, butcher,, was fined ss. and costs in cach case for having a pair of unstamped scales, and also unjust weights on December 28th. Inspector Bpence proved both cases.

ALLEGED ASSAULT ON A WOlLlN.—Thomas Water- worth of Go'borne, was charged with assaulting Mary E. Cooper. also of Go'borne, on December 6:h. Mr. Stuart defended. The complainant's evidence was to the effect that defendant and she had some unpleasantness, and he came into her house and deliberately struck her in the face. Isaac Cooper, and Edmund Twiss gave corro- borative evidence. Mr. Stuart denied that any assault had been committed ,but that his client simply prevented a friend of his from being assaulted by the Coopers. Mrs. Howarth and William Cox supported this statement, and the magistrates dismissed the case.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, TANUARY 6, 1894.

THEFT OF COAL.—George Harrison was charged with stealing 461bs. of coal, valued 3d., on December Bth, thc, property of Messrs. Richard Evans and Co. P.C. Harri- son, a constable in the employ of Messrs. Richard Evans, said that on the above date, he was on duty in the works, when he saw the defendant get upon a coal wag- gon, and throw some pieces upon the ground. He then got down and was carrying off the coal when witness arrested him. He begged to be let off as it was his first offence. The magistrates bound defendant over to ap- pear. when called upon but said that they would not be so lenient with such cases in the future.

THEFT or PmEoris.—Michael Donall, Haydeck, was charged with steeling two homing pigeons value ss. ,the property of Frederick Wm. Halton, Haydock, on Dec. 6th. Mr. Stuart, who prosecuted, said that on the above date, his client missed the pigeons and the police suc- ceeded in discovering that the defendant had taken them, and when apprehended, he did not deny having stolen them. The magistrates inflicted a fine of Sc. and costs.

A DESPERATE ANARCHIST.

CONFESSION OF THE MURDERS IN A BARCELONA

THEATRE. A LONG CHASE.

The Barcelona Anarchist Salvador Franch, or Santiago Salvador. who has been arrested at Saragossa for al- leged complicity in the Liceo Theatre outrage is a labour- ing man of no education. His wound is said to be seri- ous. He was pursaed by gentlarays for more than a week before his arrest, but continually evaded them by making his way along little-known mountain tracks. Be has made a confession which clears up many points hitherto obscure regarding the dynamite outrage at Bar- celona, when bombs were thrown amongst the people in the stalls at the Liceo Opera House, with appalling re- sults.

French, who is in the prison hospital at Saragossa be- ing treated for the wound in his side inflicted by him- self at the moment of his arrest, has informed the magistrates that he, and he alone, was the thrower of thehombs. There were two bombs, he explained, of which only one exploded. Per some reason, which he could not understand, the other failed to go off. Pos- sibly there was sonse defect in its construction. He carried them into tho theatre in his sash, in the folds of which the two infernal machines were easily concealed, for they were of small size. Taking his place in the second gallery. Franch waited quietly until the opera was approaching its conclusion and all eyes were on the stage. Then he moved put into the passage, where no- body was likely to observe him, and taking the bombs one in each hand, he hurled them with all his force down into the centre of the closely packed stalls, where the bourgeois were sitting. In the confusion and panic which followed the explo- sion, Franch, who was alrt-ady clear of the seats, had no difficulty in escaping from ths theatre,but knowing that the police, being cognisant of his Anarchist ideas,would be sure to arrest him,he made at once for a place of con- cealment in a house near the cemetery. Here he remained for twenty days, and then, deeming that the vigilance of the police would have relaxed, he left Barcelona and set out on foot for his native province of Teruel. For a short time he stayed with his own family at Castelseras, and then made his way to Saragossa to seek the hospitality of a friend living at that city. Franch averred that he ma e this full confession of his own guilt in order to save innocent people now in prison at Barcelona. As to the fabrication of the bombs, Ise had had no assistance at all. He had made them himself according to the prescribed formula. It was a matter of regret to him that so few people I had been killed at the Liceo Theatre-23 persons were killed, and about 40 injured,—because the Anarchists were resolved to exterminate the bourgeois. The prisoner further declared that he had from the first determined te commit suicide after having told the whole truth. He had undoubtedly made preparations for doing away with himself, for on him were a phial, a revolver and an enormous knife. It Is probable, how- ever,that be had made up his mind to sell his life dearly, and that he would have used his weapons on others be- fore turning thew on himself if he had had the chance.

MR MONSON AND THE WAXWORKS

EXIIIBITION.

• AN EXTRAORDINALY INCIDENT.

Mr. Monson, the chief figure in the receat Ardlamont trial, paid a visit to Birmingham on Thursday in order to protest against his figure constituting one of the ad- vertised attractions of the exhibition now staged in the Winter Gardens, Birmingham, by Louis Tussaud and Co., the waxwork modellers. He had an interview with the manager, Mr. Toinschitz, and complained that a re- presentation of him had been placed in the exhibition without his knowledge. He stated that the original Madame Tussaud Company had offered him a large sum for the privilege of exhibiting a wax figure of him, and he wanted to knew whether Louis Tussaud was prepared to offer him a similar inducement. Mr. Tomschitz replied that ne indignity had been cast upon him. Ho had not been placed in the" Chamber of Horrors," but in the chief room with the Queen on one side and the Pope Leo on the ether. They had raised no objection to being put on view,and Mr. Temschitz failed to see what grievance Mr. Monson had. Mr. Monson then stated that he wa. determined that the figure should not remain on view,and left the hall, with the intention of seeing what remedy he had against the company. The news of Monson's visit Boos spread, and created an extraordinary sensa- tion.

MIL. HAVELOCK WILSON'S AFFAIRS.

THE RECEIVING ORDER RESCINDED.

At the London Bankruptcy Court, on Thursday morn- ing, application was made t ) rescind the receiving order against Mr. J. Havelock Wilson, M.P., on the ground that all the debts had been paid in full. Receipts were handed in showing that all the debits had been paid, and the order was made and the debtor's estate reinvested in him. An order was also made upon the Official Receiver to repay the amount held by the court on the part of the " Shipping Gazette."

The visitors scored just before the call of time, through Ao.hcroft running half the length of the field, time was called, when the score stood: Penketh, 3 goals ; Fara- worth c.E.T.s., 1 goal.

POST OFFICE (WarringtOrl) V. BASEBA.T.D RESERVE.— The P. 0. were only poorly represented, but the visitors had a strong team. The game was very fast throughout and resulted in a -victory for the Post Office by two goals to one.

THE LATE MR. T. W. SPEAKMAN, OF RUITCO RN. In this month's issue of the Parish Magazine the, Vicar, the Rev. Canon Maitland Wood refers as fol- lows to the lamentable death of Mr. T. W. Spealt- man :—" It is not necessary to ask your sympathy • for the family of our good and kind friend Thomas. Wright Speakman, because I feel sure you have already given it. Mr. Speakman, who was warden at St. Michael's, and has occupied that position for neatly four years, Was a young man of such energy and promise, and one so deservedly respected, that we all feel how great a loss, not his family only, but the Church, has suffered. It will be long before he is farg-otten, and when St. Michael's becomes a strong and flourishing con- gregation, as it will, and there are officers who find its care easy and satisfactory, it is to be hoped that his name will be remembered as of one who did what be could in the earlier days." On Sunday morning the Vicar preached a sermon at St. Michael's Church with especial reference to the death of Mr. Speakmsn, who was for several years Vicar's warden of the church. The preacher based his discourse' on I. Corinthians iii, 9v; "Ye are God's building." Gathered toeether as they were that day, in that house of prayer, and bearing in mind all the things which were past, it was not p )ssible to avoid thinking with very great sorrow of the loss of one who had gone from their midst, one who in years had scarcely reached the prime of manhood ; one who he was told in that world of busines to which it had pleased God tecall him, and in which his lot was set, had shown an energy and an interest which were noticed by all who knew him, so that he was not only widely known, but widely trusted. He was one who in his family was a good son and a de- voted brother. The late Mr. Speakman, however, was best recollected by all associated with that place of wor- ship as having for nearly four years served as vicar's warden, and therefore the first among the officers of that parish. And yet he would be remembered in a manner better than that—as one found worshiping God devoutly there, and kneeling reverently before the holy table. They had a hope that as year; went by, and has his ex- perience and interest increased, he would prove a strong and useful pillar in that place, and that he would assist in building up the temple of Christ. But this was not to be. It had pleased God to take him away. After more than ten weeks of most trying sickness, he peacefully passed away on the evening of Christmas day. It was his (Canon Wood's) duty and sad pleasure to be able to administer to him, just before he died, the body and blond of Christ in that most comfortable sacrament, the Lord's Supper. Then, after he had passed away they laid his body in the cemetery near by, in the faith of the resurrection, on the day of the Holy Innocents; those who were undefiled and full of grace. The organist, Mr, C. Pownall, played the" Dead March."

OUTRAGE AT A FOOTBALL MATCH

A WELL-DE-;ERVED PENALTY.

After a recent football match at St. Albans, a specta- tor named Read accused Mr. Emslie of the Caledonia* Athletic Football Club ot foul play, and knocked him down. The S. Albans magistrates on Thursday ordered Read to pay £3 7s. 6,3. for the assault.

SUICIDE AT EIGHTY-THREE.

AN OBJECTION TO THE WORKHOUSE.

An old woman of eighty-three, living at Kirbymoor-- side, was found dead in her house, by the side of another old lady, her sister, who was speechless, but alive, and it is hoped. will recover. Both the poor old folk had, it seems, been in the workhouse, but they preferred liberty to confinement, and hence the fatal result. The jury which inquired into the case expressed their opinion that power should be given to boards of guardians "to coal- pel such aged couples and lonely persons to remain in the orkhouso."

BOUB THROWING.

BY MR. H. WHITHA.M.

" Organized charity ;Tit -have au i the more than it can do to feed the hungry and clothe the naked this winter." Paragraph's worded something like the above may now be now be read in most American papers, and, while a feeling .of satisfac- tion comes over one to know that the charitably disposed are so ordering themselves that the needs of the poor shall be met it is clearly admitted that there are more hungry and naked than can be attended to. It is stated that there aro 40,000 unemployed in Iloston. These unfortunate fellows are, it is said, " not leeches upon the public, but an element of public strength and an influence for public well-being." One would think after such a good character that these men hada natural right to a livelihood hut no, the only way out of the trouble suggested is that the" Boston merch- ant, and business man should put in their way an ocea- sional job, for which he is well able to pay." I think it needs one to be right down amongst those who get the "occasional jots" to understand just what it means. While not being quite there myself, I can honestly say that I have been treading on the' brink of the fearful precipice for the six months during which the panic has now lasted. During this time the highest weekly wages I have earned has been about 28s.—and this has varied all the way down to zero. But lam one of the fortunate ones, and am thankful accordingly. The firm I work for has made great efforts to keep all the teen at work it could, and, indeed, quite a number of men have been kept on for the mere sweet reasonable- ness of the thing, but very few, and far between have been such cases.

I have made the above personal statement so that my readers may follow the trend of my thoughts in what I have further to say, on this, a subject that has come to stay until it is radically dealt with. It is said that the number of sufferers is greater in Boston than any pre- vious winter has seen. At Ironwood,Michigan, a town of 8,000 inhabitants, 5,000 people would be starving to death were it not that a committee has Visited it and arranged to send winter clothes, food, and fuel for five months. The thermometer here registers 12° below zero, and winter has only just begun. At this place is the largest iron mine in the world, and all the men are now kilo. At another place, Bessemer, cut of 900 in- habitants 875 were found hungry and famishing. The whole town was penniless. On the day after the recent blizzard, which left the ground covered with two feet of snow, 40 men marchi.d to the hut of J. W. Bedell, road supervisor. They were all armed, some with revolvers, some with shotguns and rifles and others with pickaxes and handspikes. They demanded food, and threatened to break into the company's stores and steal it if their demands were not granted.

c Do not for a moment fancy that I am piling the agony on purposely, or selecting special cases,such cases form the chief news items in the papers just now, the very fact that in August 300 per cent.more of emigrants left this country than during the same month of 1891. This was at the beginning of the panic ; it is coi-sider- ably more now, but the immigration of Russian He- brews holds up the total of the immigration figures. Your own coal strike,happily now over, has given some fearful examples of misery, but think what it would have been had it lengthened out thrcugk the win- ter, and think what it is with us now with snow on the ground six inches deep, and no work for a large number of honest workers.

I have headed this letter with words that are startling ye L I have no great wish to write of the bomb thrower. It is enough to say with the Irish Bishop, touching the Fenian outrages that " hell is not hot enough nor eternity long enough for the punishment of such miscreants." All the same we shall have to grapple with the matter, for many of us know it to be only a result, and some of us think we know the cause. I read in my paper to-day the report of a grand opening night of the opera in New York,and it was estimated that in the two rows of boxe3 19,000,000d015. worth of jc,weis were displayed by the occupants. Allowing that at least 10,000,000d015. worth scattered over the remaining .parts of the house, for diamonds were in Profusion in the orchestra and many gems were noticed as high up as the family circle, that would make altogether a rough estimate of at least 29,000,000d015. worth of diamonds in the general dis- play. Men reading these reports will not willingly starve, and indeed, who. shall blame the unemployed if they seek to take back some of the spoil from the spoliator. In Chicago it stated that near upon 120,000 men are destitute and antiost homeless. The city halls are thrown open and hundreds seek shelter within their walls sleep- ing on the bare floors. The churches are also thrown open as sheltering places, and a committee of 100 has been formed that intends to raise £200,000 as a relict fund. . _

, The above is but a very brief sketch of the state of things here. It is not much use guessing at the immedi- ate cause of it all. At the beginning of the panic Eng- land came in well for her share of abuse. Than it was said the bank failures in Australia some three years ago was the cause of it all. At length people began to look a bit nearer home, and it was discovered that it was all on account of a Democratic Government being in power. The famous Silver Bill was repealed, and still there was no improvement. Now it is the tariff measure the Democrats are introducing. When that is removed it will be something else. It is pitiful, wondrous pitiful, going on groping like this br the cause of all our trouble. I cannot look at this great social question as one peculiar to any state, or country. Iu whatever direction the eye is turned there is to be found the unemployed question. It is in Australia as well es in England, in America as well as in Europe.

There are no longer isolated lights between employer and .employe, the Governments are stepping in atd settling the question. In six countries have been esta- blished bureaux of information, and under the prate- tion of the Government, with weekly publications dis- cussing the condition of labour. Here we have the highest testimony that the political economy of the past can no longer be made to do duty; that buying in the cheapest and selling in toe dearest market is a maxim not in accordance with the Sermon on the Mount. We have come to the point when Christian business men will have to answer the very pointed question, " can the business of the world be carried on according to Chris- tian ethics?" If not, why not? It has been said pretty often of late, and said by men of high standing that the dangerous classes are not the strikers, not the Socialists, not even the Anarc hists, but the rich manufacturers, the railway kings, the coal barons, and oil monopolists; they • are the wheat and pork 'monopolists and land syndicates. It is the money of these men that exploits the workmen at the poll atal elsewhere. Numerous cases have occurs, d here in the States of work.nen being discharged on account of their voting Democrat, and numerous petitions against tariff reforms have been sent through the mills and factories for the men to sign, or, refusing to do so, of the men being discharged. aTrade here may possibly boom with the coming of the new )eir. Should it do so, quietness may be restored tor a time. Still there is a most important matter to keep in mind, wages have been reduced very materially, and very widely over this country. This has been sub- mitted to because it was seen to be useless to "kick," also because two of the strongest trade federation were foolishly lighting one another. With the settlement of this latter stupid business and better trade will come, in my opinion, the bitterest struggles ever known in the history of labour. It is not ignorance in Russia that prompts Nihilism. it is education. The German Socialist is as a rule a scholar. In America labour journala. abound. In Eng- land they are also becoming numerous. And in England members of parliament guarantee funds for strikers. The schoolmaster has returned from his wanderings abroad. We have had him in our midst tor some years with the result that the people, by education, have raised their standard of living, and are demanding more. The theory of political and social equality has entered into every mind. If the polling booth is not clear, free, and open, as was recently stated by a writer in the "Examiner," why it shall be made so.

I go through my daily, and the monthlies pretty at- tentively, and I find there is a vastly growing number of writers, and public speakers on the platform, and ;in the pulpit demanding, not a mere " living wage," but something that will surely revolutionise the labourers position. We no longer look upon our employers as nonest men. The personal respect for employers we used to have teis been dead sonic time. It died out with the coming in (Attie Limited Liability Act. The Syndicates and stock watering cmnpauies have made of us lighters. When we read of costly stables, and kennels, and costly palaces for men, who but yesterday, were in our ranks, we know it is done with our mcney. We have been fighting hard for years to do away with sweating, and its kindred evils, now because coal barons, and others of that ilk, seek to pull down wages, it is too much to ex- pect that we shall give in quietly. Paris is in a fright to-day ; the whole of ehristendom will follow. A writer in the " Examiner " whom I very much res- pect, stated that be " objected to see the chemical workers of Widnes marching daily to Precot Work- house, some eight miles out and home to work for a pitiful Bd., to keep body and soul together." Let me re- mind him that he lays the blame altogether on the wrong shoulders. Not the miners but the vicious system under which they work was to blame. It seems to we that before this system can be abolished the misery of the past six months will be as nothing compared with what is to come,and anarchy will inevitably be the out- come of a persistence in maintaining the present social system, based as it is up•on conditions which were only possible in an age when machinery was net. I have seen statements repeatedly uring the past few months, that in every given line or business, thore is machinery suffi- cient to produce in six months all that could be con- sumed in twelve months. It is also stated by tariff re- formers that should a good measure pass Congress the American manufacturers would be enabled to flood Europe with goods, also Europe's foreign markets. We all know what the result of this will be on the wage question. It is for labour leaders to study this phrase the impstirm, ltid to educate their foPo:' erl up to the pint

LABOUR CONGRESS IN GLASGOW.

The fifth annual Conference under the auspices o. f the Scottish Labour Party was held in Glasgow on Tuesday, under the presidency of Mr. Keir Hardie, M.P. Dele- gates numbering nearly 300 were present. They repre- sented 52 trade unioes, seven trade councils, 19 branches of the Scottish Labour Party, four Henry George socie- ties, five Co-operative societies, one Ruskin society, two Nationalisation of Labour societies, and branches of the Social Democratic Federation.—The Chairman in his opening address said he was sure it would be admitted by every section represented that there was a need for a labour party in the country. More, there was need for a greater organisation of their political power than existed to-day. They had every obstacle thrown. in their way, and there was every impediment to the progress of the working classes. The party press of the country was against such a movement. The Church, generally speak- ing, was in opposition, though there were individuals amongst the well-to-do classes and in the ministerial ranks who rose superior to their surroundings, and teok their place by the side of those who were fighting for truth and righteousness. The labour party, the source of everything, was without cohesion, helpl.ss, the sport in turn of Liberal and Tory alike. He hoped that the new year upon which we had entered would see as great a development of the independent labour movement as the year which had just passed away. There might be an electi•ii before its close. If it came he hoped they would be ready for it in Scotland. He believed that in England 17 candidates had been selected; in Scotland two candidates had been chosen—Mr. Smellie for Mid- Lanark, and Professor Robertson for Cainlachie. He trusted that before long every constituency in which a candidate was to be run, and there should be fifteen or twenty in Scotland, would select their man and put him in the field, and when the election came be had no doubt that the result would be widely different from what it had been on previous occasions.—(Cheers.)—The meet- ing afterwards discussed a lengthened programme of resolutions bearing on various phases of the labour move- ment. The first one, which was unanimously f;:arried, expressed satisfaction with the progress which the cause of labour was making, as was evidenced by the rapid growth of the independent labour party in politics, and by the resolution of the Trade Union Congress in favour of an independent labour party for securing the nationa- lisation of the land and the industrial capital of the nation. 'f he motion also called on all workers in Scot- land to support any organisation which had for its ob- ject the return to Parliement of representatives who will act irrespectively of the convenience of any political party in securing justice to labour and the establishment of a just social order. Resolutions were adopted urging on the Government the necessity, first, of appointing as factory inspectors women who have worked in a work- shop, factory, or warehouse, and, second, of extending the franchise to every person of 21 years of age and over. The conference further. counselled the Govern- ment not to allow themselves to be bullied by the House of Lords into allowing contracting out of the terms of the Employers' Liability Act, and declared that the present misery and destitution resulting from people being un- employed could only be solved by a reorganieatiou of the industrial system ca a socialistic basis.

BANKRUPTS' WIVES.

It is becoming quite a common occurrence in small bankruptcies (r,:marks the " Drapers' Record '') for the bankrupt's wiry to find the money for the purchase of the husband's estate,arrange with the landlord to become the tenant of the shop, and suddenly blossom into a trader. This is one of the effects of the Married Women's Property Act which gives'so much anxiety to trustees in bankruptcy. Of course it often happens that the wife gives more for an estate than it would be likely t) realise is sold by tender to a stranger, or if offered by public auction. But the unsatisfactory part about the proceeding is the suspicion which it raises that it is really the bankrupt himself who is buying the stock with his creditors' money. He con'!inues to live with his wife, carries on the bw se ;4 in her name, and t. the outside world there is pre( eus little to distinguish the change of ownership which has been effected. It is easy to see how abuses may arise, and how commercial morality is likely to be affected by proceedings of this kind, which trustees ought to investigAte most thoroughly. But the fact that a fair or even a good price has been offered for an estate usually seems to outweigh all other considerations whereas it might sometimes be found that the bankrupt was not only pulling the strings, but supplying the money out of " a stocking " which the purchase of the estate by no means empties.

PRINCIPLE V. EXPERIENCE.

- - - " Let us do evil that good may come? Whose condemnation is just." Romans iii. 8. Sir,—As an intellectual exercise the following of "Common Justice "through the illogical windings of his epistles and the attempt to unravel the moral entangle- ment therein, " licks into fits " the charades, acrostics and conundrums of which we have just had so plentiful a crop. They who sit at the feet of this ex- ponent of expediency may know what a " bad old ethical princtple" is. Ido not. When I see a good bad man, or a black white man, I will ask him to explain this mystery. To hear this man there might be no settled moral truths—none that is that could not be set aside it they inconvenienced him. With slight alteration his arguments in palliation of the crime of intimidation might have been used by Mr. Yagin to his "young friends" in extenuation of their little peccadilloes. Your correspondent, like many more, make expedience the rule of life, in place of the better rule of principle. As a consequence, much mischief ensues. Thus—l am heir, say, to large estates, one life only intervening. It might seem expedient to remove this obstacle ; but a "bad old ethical principle" forbids, and this finds solid embodiment and forceful effectiveness in the man it, blue. If we cannot compass our ends without breach of znoral laws we have no right to attempt it. And now, Mr. Brouchemite. Largely agreeing with this gentleman's contentions I am yet not oblivious to the flaws in the diamond. fie appears to be suffering from Socialism on the brain, and confounds Mr. Daltoa's bus:bear—Rationalism—with Socialism. Not all Ration- alists are Socialists, nor are all Socialists rational. But what I want to call our cosmopolitan friend's attention to is this—How does his endorsement of the golden rule: "Do unto others as ye would they should do uhto you and, " Let there be liberty all round," how do these ex- cellent maxims agree with his doctrine of intimidation ? Liberty ! Well, yes, liberty, qualified by S. Woods, and Co. ,but no freedom of contract between masters and the extra-raining British Public.—Yours, JOHN PEERS.

DISESTABLISIISIENT. air,—" Every man has a right to his own- religion." §O.as you esteemed correspondent " Ituncornian," tailing from the fret fr We3t, and, with three or four exceptions, every member oi your clielltele will cordially echo those words of" Ituncornian," We have in this country a political institution called a " Church as by law established." 'This secular church is state protected in a legalised robbery of upwards of £6,000,000 annually, from the poor of England and IN ales alone, to say nothing of the Scotch department. The real legatees of this money are our unfortunate fel- low countrymen who are dying of starvation in thous- ands annually, and starving on workhouse skill in scores of thousands. The actual recipients are a hand- ful of state officials called " Priests," including prelates with their palaces, horses and hounds ; everyone of yf bich " priests " is sworn in his so-called " ordination" oath to practices at variance with the expressed wishes of the donois. To redress this gigantic reif, so scandal- ous to the honour of a Christian country, an enligh- tened Liberal ministry, headed by that diet n- guished peer of nature's noblemen, Mr. Gladstone, has brought forward an initiatory measure known popu- larly as the " Welsh Sespensory Bill." Ths State Church Stipendiaries, backed up tiy their hereditary ac- complices, the state receivers of land, are Up in arms against the prospect of being called upon to restore their ill-gotten spoils. Every Anglican Conventicle in the country has been resounding with denunciation against this " gross cowardly and iniquitous" attempt to give to the peed wl-iat loslazes to them. Throughout the length and breadth of the land, lecture halls have been en- gagt d from the Albert and Exeter Halls down to the smallest and most obscure village meeting rooms, by paid retailers of false history, and subsidized" Defence" 1 pamphleteers. Those modern Ephesian silversmitLs,l known as Protestant " Evangelical " alliance men, have valiantly come forward as henchmen in the disgraceful crusade against humanity and righteousness, foreknow- ing instinctively, "Like those sore sick, who hear a passing bell," that with disestablishment comes equality and justice, and that " their trade will be injured thereby." In this juncture, the Government fighting night and day against an obstruction unparalleled in our political his- tory and unable to spare a single man from his post in the house, appeals for the support of w kat Mr.,Gladstone once called" outside pressure." "Disunion among your opponent% and security for yourself" is a time-honoured truism, and it has been a settled policy with the State Church from its founda- tion, to make for itself unconscious links and buttresses of the free churches, by fomenting discord and laugh- , ing in its sleeve while they were wasting their aggres- sive strength against each other. In virulently abusing Catholicism, which is the mother of free churches,Juda- ism, which for all practical purposes in this country is a race rather than a religicn,and the Greek and Moham- medan churches which don't concern us in the slightest, without one word of protest against that monstrous hypocritical anachronism, the political "Church," soffe correspondents prove themselves to be (consciously or otherwise) expert allies of it. Iw- it to conclude with the expression of a hope that every Liberal Club and AsBOciatiOn in your district will show their opinion of the -sectarian animosity censers by sending up unanimous resolutions to the Government in support of the Welsh Bill, and thus give a word of cheer to the Hawarden Chieftain and his lleagues in their arduous battle against falsehood, fraud, and corruption.—Faithfully yours,

WM. F. COMBER.

Knowles Hill, Lewisham.

CHESHIRE LINES ENGINEERS SIGNAL DEPART- MENT ANNUAL DINNER. ATHLETICS.

RCGIIT FooTBALL.

The match with Rugby on Saturday, with which War- rington finished up the year 1893, was a most remarkable one, Warrington making a record score for the Waders- pool ground. 'The Rugby players were a big lot 01 fellows, but they have a great deal to learn in football. Uhey led off with a rush, and for a time, they appeared to have the upper hand of Warrington, but after the first quarter of an hour, some one of the Warrington players was almost repeatedly getting the bah over their line. Th, members of the Warrington team in fact, had so much of their own way, that, at times, they actually truggled with one another, as to who should score the try, and fleesom found his position at full back such a , sinecure that he went in for scoring himself. z

A new recruit from Stockton Heath in the person cf Barber, bids fair to become a splendid player. his speed .And dodging abilities, being of a high order. By the way, the Stockton Heath team has a good number of promising players, and on Monday, Broady and Barber had a large share in the defeat of the St. Helens Recs. A team. But to return to Saturday's match. A contem- porary was asking on Saturday when Warrington would find a goal kicker, for they tried, tried and tried, but they never got more than a try. Warrington was .evi- dently determined to show that they were not in want of a good kicker, for out of ten attempts, Evans kicked seven goals, while Nevins landed three in three attempts. A score of 59 points to nil, which make a great lift in the averages at the end of the season.

-- Fresh from their victory over Rugby, the Warrington team journeyed for the second time this season, to St. Helens on N;.vr Year's Day to play the formidable Recreation Club of that town. The first meeting of the teams resulted in a win for Warrington by two tries to one try. The Recs. strengthened their team by the in- clusion of l'ilkington, who obtained his " Blue" this season. Although the match resulted in a draw of a try each, Warrington fully deserved to win, for they kept the Recs. on their own quarters for the greater part of the game, especially in the last half. This •shows that %arrington have a superior stamina than most teams, for they generally hold out the longest. In fact it was only the excellent defence of the Recs. that saved them fum defeat.

Tlic match between Lancashire and Cheshire,in which three of the Warrington players were engaged, resulted in a win for Lancashire by eleven points to three, thus obtaining f•-,r the county, the- championship of the North-Western division of counties. The match in ques- tion was spoiled by the fog„which enveloped the ground so much that it was with great difficulty that the play could be seen. The two Bates played very well from all accounts, as also did Jolley.

The A team on Monday simply walked round the St. Helens Recs. A, as the score of two goals four tries to nil indicates. This was not expected, for as a rule the Recs. are a tough lot, and have frequently lowered the Warrington colours. It will be remembered that a few years ago, they were the only team to vanquish the Warrington players during a whole season. 'the two Stockton Heath players played an excellent game, and contributed to the scoring.

ANTECEDENTS. Last week our sketch was of Mr. John T.Bate,this week we give "Smack "or James T. Bate, his companion at half back. Although their surnames are alike it should be stated that they are in no way related to each other. " Smack's" first training ground was with the Sankey Club, whose field was situated in Lovely-lane. This was about six years ago, and after one season's play with the Sankey Club he joined the Latchford True Blues, a club which John T. Bate also played for. But" Smack's" sterling,' qualities as a player at half back were noticed, and in the following year, 1890, he commenced playing in the Warrington "A " team. Four matches with this team were enough to warrant him a position in the first team, and accordingly be was given a place at half back with the other J. T. Bate. The two work well to- gether, and to them the Warrington team owe many victories.

Perhaps no-Warrington player has made his way into the Laneashire County team quicker than has "smack" Bate, for in the following year after playing in the first team he received County honours twice, and also in tha year 1892 two similar honours were conferred upon him. In the year which has just closed he fulfilled three county engagements, but it is very probable he would have re- ceived more had he not for some weeks been suffering from a sprained ankle, when two county matches were played. The three matches in which he engaged were with Cumberland. Yorkshire, and Cheshire, the latter match being played last Saturday. His play for the county has received much commendatien, and un- doubtedly he will he again chosen, and it is not unlikely that ere long he will be among, the ranks of international playerS.

THE GYMNASIUM. Matters have been rather tame, of late, in cz-mnectiOn with the " Gym." But I'm glad to bear that the "strings are being worked again." On Saturday, the 13th,a dis- play is to be given. The wonder, and the pity, of it is that one or two, have not been given before,this season. A great niany of the public wonder why the fortnightly entertainments, which were so popular two years ago, are not revived. Of course it is very impertinent for the public to indulge in " wondering "like that, but still, I should be half inclined to commit the same transgression were I the public, Anyhow, it is pleasing to know that when the display does take place, it will he worthy of witnessing for in addition to several other interesting features there is going to be a tug-of-war between a gym- nasium and a football club team.

The other day I 'Flj affOrded Tin opportunity of seeing an imtance of tbi:i ability of the conductor, Mr. Kinsley, in inatt?.?g appertaining to theoretical gymnastics. Most 1 athletes will be aware of the machine known as the Home Exercise Machine. There are two generally in , use, hut no great satisfaction is given by either of them ' because of the bar which couples the two lines together ' and which prevents anything like a free use of the indi- vidual lines. An alteration has been made upon these machines by Mr. Kinsley, with the result that a most valuable improvement has been achieved. Upon in- specting the improved article I was considerably struck by its extra advantages, and was not surprised to find that the expert gymnasts of the institution preferred it to the machines by better known makers. Some one ought to boom Mr. Kinsley's work, if it is only for the benefit of those indulging in home gymnastics.

By the way the N.P.R.S. competition for the 200 1 guineas shield has commenced. Warrington have been drawn in the second round to meet Newport, and it is Possible that if they are successful, they will next meet last rear's opponents, Dolobran. I am told that if this meeting does take place Warrington are now about pre- pared to administer" Boston beans," to that aggressive Birmingham team. The exercises, however, are not looked upon with much favour by those who admire skilled gymnastics.

WARRINGTON RURAL SANITARY AUTHORITY.

The monthly meeting of this Authority was held yesterday in the Board room at the Worknouse. The Rev. G. F. Roberts occupied the chair, and there were also present: Messrs. John Houghton, A. Jolley, J. Swinton, Smith, Mather, S. Sutton (clerk). Dr. Sephton (medical officer), Mr. F. Ker- foot (inspector), R. Stone (representing Mr. W. Owen, surveyor). CANAL BOATS ACT. The Clerk informed the Authority that he had re- ceived the usual application for the annual report to be made to the Local Government Board under the Canal Boats Act, 1884. He said it was a matter which was generally alludedto by the Inspector and himself. They would accordingly report as usual. MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT. Dr.Sephton reported that the number of births was 42, deaths 18. The death rate was equal to 16 4 per 1000 against 17.4 in the corresponding month of last year. rhere were eight cases of scarlet fever, and one case of enteric fever. Mr. Wm. Houghton : Generally speaking Sir, I suppose the repoyt is a favourable one.—Dr. Sephton : Yes, scarlet fever is the only thing we have to contend with at present.—rhe Chairman : Can you account for so much scarlet fever prevailing in the dis- trict ?—Dr. Sephton said it appeared they had had the disease in Cadishead and at Hollins Green schools. He also thought the disease existed at the school before it was officially reported.—Mr. William Houghton: It is in the Workhouse schools, I believe.—Dr. Sephton : Yes, it exists throughout the district.—The Chairman: Is there no stopping it P—Dr. Sephton replied that every possible precaution was being adopted to prevent the spread of the disease and carbolic disinfectants were being freely used. It was a fact that if any person suffering from the disease wilfully exposed himself or herself in the streets they could take legal proceedings against them.

THE SU RVIZ YO rt'S I ',mum. The Clerk mentioned that owing to illness their Sur- veyor. Mr. William Owen, was unable to be present to submit his usual report.

Trouble has again arisen at Lord Mashatn's Pit, Featherstone, owing to seventy men being discharged for not resuming work on New Year's Day. The miners' officials have been communicated with. The principals who figured in the Ardlacoont trial will, it is stated, reappear in the law courts in fresh charac- ters. Great satis- faction has been expressed in Warrington that a War- rington vessel should have the honour of heading the cargo vessels on the canal. TEN THOUSAND PASSENGERS FROM LIVERPOOL. It is Stated 10,000 persons or thereabouts left the Prince's Landing stage, Liverpool, on Monday morning, in nine steamers for Manchester, and between half-past eight and ten the stage was crowded by holiday makers and others struggling to get on board the boats. With few exceptions, those who went on board were .people from Manchester, Warrington, and other places along the Canal. They poured into Liverpool by early special excursions, arriving chiefly at the Central Station, be- tween which place and the Plerhead a number of wagonettes and 'buses were engaged. In addition to this there was a large number of excursion trains to Latchford, and at one time, during the morning, there 1 was to be seen a line of coaches reaching almost from Arpley Station to Latchford Station.

steamed away from the locks with a dozen cat in her waits, there was great cheering from the thousands who lined the bank, and the uproar from a score of steam whistles and syrens was deafening. The sailing ship . Sophia Wilhelmina was lying outside the locks still dis- mantled, and looking anything but ready for a" show." The opinion was expressed that it would be very seldom that the Manchester people would see such ships floating in their docks, because of the great difficuity Ithere is in steering them along a narrow waterway. From Latchford to Manchester people almost con- tinuously lined the banks of the canal and the cheering, and waving of handkerchiefs and bannerettes, drew forth the remark that the Rowland fleet was having a triumphal progress. Certainly at times the progress was slow, and the stoppages in the leeks somewhat lengthy, but there was much to admire in the splendid bridges and in that feat of engineering skill, the Barton aqueduct. The scenery along the banks can- I not be described as picturesque, being for the most part fiat and bare. With his customary kindness Mr. Rowlands had taken precautions that his numerous passengers should not suffer from hunger or . thirst. There was a plentiful supply of both eatables ! and liquids, which enabled all to bear the cold and fatigue of the journey with an equanimity and comfort which would otherwise have been impossible. After passing Mode Wheel Locks there is a magnificent sheet of water to Manchester, but unfortunately it is some- what inky in colour, and emits, when agitated, an odour that cannot he compared to the spices of Arabia. In fact, some of the Whines scents are preferable. At half past throe the Owen Tudor moored alongside a wharf n Salford Docks and the rest of the fleet arrived within half an hour later. Everybody expressed themselves

highly pleased with the trip, and before the numerous company separated three cheers were given for Mr. and , Mrs. Rowlands with much heartiness. The re- turn journey- to Runcorn was made by train. About 35 of the company on the Cecil proceeded to the Merchant's Hotel, in Old-ham-st., where a capital dinner was partaken of.—Mr. Janaes Littler occupied the chair, and proposed "Success to the Ship Canal," a toast which was drunk with enthusiasm.—Mr. C. Timmins proposed success to Messrs. Brundrit and Co., which was honoured with " three times three," and the Chairman suitably responded. The Runcorn pilots have been- busily engaged on the Ship Canal this week. Captain Hilt. piloted the S.S.
