THE OPENING CEREMONY.

Any ceremonial, no matter how stately, with which the opening of the Manchester Ship Canal may be cele- brated a few months hence will fail to efface from the recollection of those who saw the great popular de- monstration of Monday. The spectacle was worthy of the occasion and characteristic of the people. The de- sire of the directors to make the affair as quiet as pos- sible, in view of the ceremony which they promise later in the year, had been honoared to some extent. Few buildings were decorated. In Manchester, during the day here and there a flag tloated,but generally speaking there was no sign in the streets that the sounding city was now in truth to hear '6 The great thunder of our is- land shore." On the quays at Salford and Old Trafford, on the banks of the canal from Mode Wheel to Barton, and from Barton to Latchford the scene was very diffe- rent. Under a sky which anticipated the promise of spring au enormous concourse of people was assetibled, stretching in almost unbroken line down either side of the great waterway. Every part of Lancashire was re- presented. fhe intense and earnest enthusiasm of the people was only less remarkable than the perfect good order with which they governed their innumerable ranks. The city and county police had little to do but to guard forbidden paths,and with their usual discretion they made their presence as little felt as possible. To the vigilance of the .combined force, however, must,in large measure be ascribed the happy immunity from accident which favoured the day's proceedings. The scenes of animation, which will long be remem- bered by Warringamians, commenced at Latchford, on Sunday. All day long the people came in thousands, fram "early morn till" dewy eve," and then thousands came again to see the marvellous sight of a feet of vessels lying within a stone's throw of Thelwall Church, and on a site which was once a sequestered rural spot. The New Year's Eve was such as the people of Warrington had never dreamed of. About a quarter of an hour before mid- night a ship's rocket went up, and exploded with a loud report. Immediately every steamer in the place began blowing whistles and clanging bells. For miles around the din could be heard. As soon as it was daylight many thousands of Warrington and neighbouring resi- dents, who knew the best points of the districts, had assembled ou the Lancashire side of the canal and stretched away through Thelwall and beyond Warbur- ton. Train after train brought more thousands from Manchester, Bolton, Blackburn, Wigan, Leigh, and other places, and many of these made their way to the boats in the lockznwhich before ten o'clock were crowded with delighted passengers. At the Manchester end it was found necessary td reserve almost all their accom- modation available for thO' shareholders and their friends The shareholders alone number 40,000, and of these a very large percentage wished either to be present or to have the gift of tickets among their personal friends. If admission had been free and unrestricted it is almost certain that not less than half-a-million persons would have crowded on the banks of the canal between Man- chester and Barton; even as it was there appeared to be close upon 100,000 between the two points. lit morn- ing was beautifully fine for the time of the year, but the crowd soon found notwithstanding the appearane: of the sun, that there was a nipping-wind. For most of the spectators the wait until the time appointed was a very trying one, and this had the effect of chilliog their enthusiasm.

THE SCENE AT LATCEIFORD. As thehour for the departure of the vessels approached the scene at. Latchford because perfectly bewildering. The occasion was observed as a general holiday in town, and to pervert an old saving " Warrington and his wife,"—and children too—came forth to witness the spectacle. But besides Warringtonians, there crowded to the scene thousands of persons from Liverpool, Man- chester, Wigan, and other towns, and before ten o'clock the whole neighbourhood ware in some sense of the word, a perfect pandemonium. Knutsford-road, from Bridge- street to Latchford Locks, was simply packed with a slowly mowing multitude of interesting spectators. In the multitude was sandwiched a long line of vehicles which stretched its length right up te the loclis. But the real scene of excitement was going on along each side of the waterway from the Thelwall-road Swing Bridge to the locks. here people were simply jambed together—it was almost an impossibility to move. For hours these thousands of people stood and waited pati- ently, unlieedful of the biting cold which chilled the air. It was a sight which no one who witnessed will soon forget. Ns matter at what elevation one might view the spectacle, the result was the same. Faces, faces were everywhere; they appeared to be in myriads, and all bore the same look of expectant aninsation. As the vessels were seen preparing to leave their moorings, the excitement became intense ; indeed it seemed even vastly out of proportion to the occasion, great as it was. Shortly after ten o'clock the Norseman, on board of which were the directors of the Ship Canal Company, was unmoored. And then arose a veritable bahal of sounds—a wild confusing conglomeration of shrieking ' bellowing whistles, and shouts of enthusiasm. To a calm enlooker, t— - her - acene for a few momenta was temarkable. It was simply pathetic to see the various / attempts to give vent to the feeling. Some people could be heard singing " God save the Queen," others " Brit- annia Rules the Waves," wlelst others went further afield and chose songs of joy not quite so appropriate. or fully ten minutes the spectacle, with its attendant 1 demonstrations, was simply confusing. But as the vessels moved away, the excitement diminished. Following the Snowdrop was the Crocus, on board of which were a large number of relatives and friends of officials of the Manchester Corporation. Then came the °vat 13r11:ain with the ,F3ilFarti (::,:rporatign, the Brackley carrying the officials of the Ship Canal,and the Dagrnar,a, hoard of which were the staff of the Bridgewater Canal. On board the Crocus we noticed Mr. Pernber, Q.C., Mr. Joseph Bright, M.P., and Mr. H. M`Neil. Mr. Leese, M.P., Mr. J. W. Hamilton, and other noted Manchester citizens were on board the Snowdrop. The Lord Mayor, Sir John Elarwood, and the other directors were con- veyed on the Norseman from Latchford to Mode Wheel. There the Lord Mayor, Mr. Alderman Bailey (Mayor of Salford), the Lady Mayoress, and Mr. Pember, Q.C., came on board the Snowdrop, and that boat was the 1 erg one to enter the Docks at Pomona. THE WARRINGTON CORPORATION TRIP. The members of the Warrington Corporation having decided to take an official trip with the triumphal pro- cession to Manchester on Monday, Messrs. Monks, Ball and Co., generously placed their steamer, the Helvetia, at their disposal. According to the arrangements at first , made, it was intended to start from the Liverpool Lanel- ing Stage, but it was afterwards decided to commence from Warrington. A large party left the Town Hall by 'bus at 2.30 a.m., and proceeded to Tom Paine's bridge, and embarked on the Helvetia, which was moored in the creek which at some future date will form the entrance to the Warrington docks. The Helvetia was under the command of Captain Joseph Monks, and was gaily de- corated with bunting, and in large letters on canvas was the word ' Warrington," which was intended to informs the spectators who lined the banks of the fact that the steamer had on board the representatives of War- rington. The following gentlemen embarked : Mr. F. Monks, J.P., C.C., Aldermen Biackhurst, Francomb, Wright, Sutton and Ashton ; Councillore Monks, Smet- hurst, Flood, Plunapton, Shaw-Green, Shaw, Bonnell, Shillcock, Hutchinson,Bennett, Hepherd, Heaton, Long- ton, Skelton, Garner, Cannell, Bolton, Tinsley, and Hill, Rev. F. K. Freeston, Messrs. J. Lyon Whittle (town clerk), Stanley Wilson (deputy town clerk), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), T. Lon,edin (borough surveyor), Haddock (gas manager), S. Hunt (chief constable), In- spector Spitike, H. B. White (magistrates' clerk),(i. F.F. White (deputy magistrates' clark),J. H. Fleming (repre- sentative of the Chamber of Commerce), E. Place (mar- ket inspector), F. Turner (superintendent fire brigade), R. Norman (nuisance inspector), G. Riley (school atter:. ' pence officer), Leigh (cemetery registrar), Taylor (gas department), Powys (Town Hall keeper), DePrez, C. Twice, Ratlibone, R. G. Flenshall, and Hall. At nine o'clock, the Helvetia proceeded to the Latchford locks to take her place in the procession. After a short delay in the locks, during which aephotograph of the vessel was , taken by Mr. BirVes, she commenced her jeurney to 1 Manchester amid much cheering from the enormous num- ber of Warringtonians who lined the banks on both sides for two or three miles. The Helvetia should have preceded the America and Chanticleer in the procession, but for some cause, these two vessels got out of the locks first, and they refused to give way. At the Irlam locks, owing to a misunderstanding, the Helvetia was crowded almost to the last by a number of other vessels. The canal is, however, a good width above the locks, and amid much chaering,the Warrington vessel quickly passed her rivals with the exception of the two vessels above named, before Barton Locks were reached. She obtaineds a favourable position in the locks, and when they were left, there was a fine race between her and the Chantic- leer, in which the Warrington boat won. There was now only the America to pass in order that the Helvetia should , get her proper position, and the America was evidently determined to keep the lead. The Helvetia, however, proved to be the speedier boat, and a loud shout pro- claimed the fact that she had passed her rival. The banks from this point, were lined almost the whole of the distance to Manchester with crowds of spectators who greeted the Warrington boat most enthusiastically as she passed. The Mode Wheel locks were quickly passed, and the .Helvetia soon got to her destination at Salford docks, where there was an immense crowd. On arriving at the landing stage, Councillor Smethuret 'I called upon the people to give " Three cheers for the ' Ship Canal," which were lustily given, followed by cheers for NVarrington and Salford. Alderman Fran- eostb, on behalf of the Corporation then proposed a vote of thanks to Messrs. Monks, Hall and Co. for having placed that splendid vessel at their disposal. He believed everybody had enjoyed the trip. That Canal would mean a lot of good for Warrington, and he hoped those on board would share in it. The trip that day had undoubtedly been a great success.—Alderman Wright briefly seconded, and the motion was heartily carried, " For they are hearty good fellows" was sung and cheers given.—Cr. F. W. Monks, in the alesence of nis father, replied, and remarked that should occasion arise. the firm would always be prepared to do as they had dans that day. (Applause.)

A PATHETLIC INCIDENT.

A great deal of sympathy was felt at Latchford in connection with the s:ad accident which cccurred at about ten o'clock on I•aturday night, and which de- prived Mr. Platt of the chief engineer of the Norseman, who had been in Mr. Platt' service 23 years. It seemed that the poor fellow, whose name was Benjamin Row- bottom, and who resided at Garth Ferry, Bangor, North Wales, was making his way to the yacht along with his mate, Mr. Fry, when in the darkness he steppod off the quay into the water. Rowbottom was about 45 years of age and was married.

THE PEARSON AND KNOWLFS COAL AND IRON

COMPANY.

The Pearson gnd Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Limited, Warrington, have done a great deal of work for the Ship Canal Company since the first sod of the great undertaking was cut. They made and fixed the iron required in the bridge over the Runcorn and Latch- ford Canal at Latchford ; the bridge carrying the Lon- don and North-Western Railway over Bradshaw-lane. Latchford ; the bridge carrying the Altrinchain road over the London and North-western railway at Thelwall a three span bridge over the Cheshire lines st Glazebrook a bftige over the Bridgewater canal at Old Traflord ; a bridge over. the Cheshire Lines Railway at the same place ; a trellis foot bridge over the Cheshire lines at Flixton ; the bridvs for the approach roads to the Part- • ington coal basin, eight in all ; all the foot bridges for the estuary locks and entrance gates, as well as all the iron and steel fittings for the lock gates at Eastham, Latchford, 'clam. Barton and Manchester. In addition to the above work the steam navvies have been repaired and gearing made for them. A coal tip constructed at East ham, a bridge made over Randle's sluice, and the cast iron lining of the subway under the bed of the Ship Canal at Barton have just been finished. A large quan- titv of (thee work has been accomplished, including the manufacture of two 10 ton locomotive steam cranes, and many hundrud sets of points and crossings, the total weight of the iron, used being considerably over 5,000 tens.

A WARBINGTON LABOURER DROWNED. At eleven o'clock on Wednesday, the body of a man was seen floating on the Ship Canal between Wash-lane, Highlevel Bridge and Twenty Step Bridge, Stockton Heath, Warrington, arid was towed to the bank at the latter bridge. It was conveyed to the Red Lion Inn and examined by Constable Brewis. It had apparently been in tke water for a considerable length of time. it was the body of a medium stout man, of indefinate age, dressed in black serge jacket and vest, and cordu- roy trousers. In the pockets were a silver watch and chain and 75.--Further investigations showed that the body was that of a labourer named Thomas Colmar, aged 19, who had been in the employ of Mr. Broady, farmer, Latch- ford. The deceased had been missing since the 27th ot November. On that day he had left the farm t) see a friend off to Ireland, by a train leaving Bank Quay late at night. He was seen to commence the return journey with a number of companions, but he left them at War- "rington Bridge, and was never heard of afterwards. Strange to say, when the body was recovered the de- ceased's right hand was in his right hand trousers pocket. Mr. Worsley held an inquest upon the body of the deceased, in the Red Lion Inn,yesterday. The brother of deceased said that he had left him on Warrington Bridge, at about 10-30 on the night of November 27. He went towards home along Wilderspool Causeway, and witness had never seen him since. Deceased had had some drink, but was not drunk.--P.C. Brew said that there was a mark upon the forehead, of decemed, butj , in his opinion, it had been inflicted after death.—The !jury returned a veidict of " Found Drowned."

THE rola OF MANCHESTER: RUNCORN ANNULLED. The" London Gazette" of Tuesday night contains at warrant of the Lords of Commissioners for her Majesty's Treasury, declaring the limits of the port of Liverpool. The warrant also revokes and annuls the appointment of Runcorn as a port, and includes" what is now that port in the litnite of the intended port of Manchester, but it is not our intention that tliis last mentioned ex- tension of the intended port of Manchester shall be deemed to affect any of the rights, power, or privileges of the port of Liverpool, or of the Mersey Docks and Harbour Board, in relation to the waters over which the said board have powers, under the provisions of the Mersey Docks Acts Consolidation Act, 1858, or any other 1001 Act." As regatas Manchester, the warrant declares :—" And we do hereby appoint Manchester, on and after the Ist day of January, 1394, to las a port of the United King- dom called England, and do hereby declare that the limits of the said port shall commence at the eastern ter- mination of the part of Liverpool; that is to say, at an imaginary straight line across the river Mersey from Dungeon Point, on tke Lancashire shore, to Ince Ferry on the Cheshire shore, and shall include' the river Mersey, above the said lino and the river Irwell so far as the same are navigable, and the river Weaver to Frodsharn Bridge ; and shall also include the Manchester Ship Canal from the entrance thereof at East- ham, in the county of Chester (where it touches the port of Liverpool), to Hunt's Bank. in the city and parish of Manchester, with all channels, havens, streams, creeks, cuts, and docks within the limits aforesaid. And we do hereby, from the 31st day of December, 1893, annul all further limits of the said ports of Liverpool and Runcorn and declare Runcorn to be no loner a port. SHIP CANAL EXCITEMENT. Mr. Henry Whitworth died at the Grange. Eccles, on Tuesday night. He had been ill for sometime, and his great fear latterly was that he would not live to see, or at any rate to know of, the actual opening of the Man- Chester Ship Canal. The excitement of this event was certainly the iininedia.te cause of his death. FROM RUNCORN co MANCHESTER. The London" Gazette" of 'Tuesday announced that Runcorn was no longer a Port. The people of the" good old Port" were aware of this fact before this official in- timation to the world, but it did not prevent a large number of them from feeling great interests in the open- ing of the Ship Canal on New Year's Day. If the Com- missioners had not thought fit to follow the example set by the Corporation of Warrington, taking part in the auspicious event, there were two Runcorn trading firrne, determined to have a share in the opening of the Port of Manchester, namely, Messrs. J. Brundrit and Co., and Mr. W. Rowlands. The latter gentleman who supplied upwards of a million tons of gravel during the construc- tion of the Manchester Ship Canal, had three steamships bound for Salford Docks, the Owen Tudor, the Hebe, and the Lady Kate, carrying respectively 210 tons, 150 tons, and 100 tons of granite setts from the Penmaen- mawe Quarries of Messrs. Brundrit. 'The Cecil also car- ried 230 tons of similar material from the owners' quar- ries. Mr. Rowlands had invited a number of friends and business colleagues to make the voyage, and at half past eight on Monday there was a large crowd assem- bled at the Old Quay, whence a start was to be made. ;On the Owen Tudor were Mr. and Mrs. Rowlands, Mr. W. E. Rowlands, Mr. Frank Rowlands, Mr. and Mrs. R. Abel, Mr. W. Ilankinson,Mr. John Laurence (Lloyd's agent), Mr. W. H. Weston, Mr. Richards, Mr. Richard Hough (Winsford), Captain LeCouteer,Mr. T. H. Annett, . Mrs. Hughes, Mrs. John Wilson, Mr. W. B. Crossley and Me. Fred Lewis, Captain Lewis; Mr. U. E. Sayce, Mr. I. Carr, and Mr. 11. White, of Widnes, joined the ship at Latchford Locks. S. Tas for, It. J. Grice, H. White, NV. Lancaster, 11. Branch, W. Sutton, G. Horsefield, J. Horsefield, M. Barker, A. Evans, J. Evans, A. Linnell, J. Swinton, A. R. Bradshaw, Jas. Littler and others. at five minutes past nine, the Owen:Tudor steamed away from the dock side under the command of Captain Js. Ravenscrolt, followed by the three vessels already ri named and several others including the Dynamic from IBelfast. 'The Hebe was in CoMmaritl of Captain George Harvey, and the Lada Kate was in charge of Captain Daniel Atherton. The Cecil was piloted by Captain H. Hill senior, Captain George Hardley, being in command. The vessels gaily decorated with streamers and bunting made quite a brilliant show as they steamed along in the brilliant sunshine, and it being quite clear that Latchford would be reached too late to take part in the official procession, this contingent was quickly and appropriately named " 'Rowlands procession." It was an ideal wintere day for toe purpose, and although the wind blew keen and cold, voyagers kept the deck, in order to get a complete view of the new waterway. The strata of rock through which we had to pass to Latch- ford was examined with great interest, and it was noticed that in places there were great rents and that in others there were signs of crumbling away. On arriv- ing at the swing bridge at Wilderspool the pedestrian and vehicular traffic was so great, that the pro- gession was stopped " for twenty minutes. An 1 entrance was made into Latchford Locks I shortly before eleven o'clock, and the engineer of the party ascertained that it occupied the apace of seven minutes to fill the large locks. FATALI TY AT WESTON POINT.

On Thursday afternoon, at the Weaver Hotel, Mr. J. E. Worsley, coroner held an inquest on the body of George "rarbuck. aged 85 years, of Barnton, Northwich, who met with his death on Monday nizht under the fol- lowing distressing circumstances. Ta,rbuek was captain of the fiat Toiler, ofLiverpool, which was moored along- side the Ship Canal Wall in the new basin at Weston Point. About eleven o'clock at night he was seen going on lioard by a - lampman named Richard Collins, who, knowing the difficulty of the road, watched him get to his destination. He saw him get upon the ladder to de- scend, which twistinp.; caused him to fall between the fiat and the canal wall. Collins, in going to his rescue, was similarly treated by the ladder. In answer to his cries for help to members of the crew of the Falmouth Castle came two his assistance and got him out of the water 'in a very exhausted condition. Information was given to the police, who began to drag for the body of Tarbuck, but only succeeded in finding it shortly before nine o'clock on Tuesday nsorning. A verdict of "acci- dental death " was returned, and the Coroner compli- mented Collins on his conduct. -

THE REV. PETER MACKENZIE• AT WARRINGTON. There were large congregations at Bold-st., Wesleyan Chapel, on Sunday morning and evening, to hear the Rev. Peter Mackenzie preach. It is some considerable time since the rev, gentleman visited Warrine,rton, and much interest was aroused by his visit. A lecture was also given by him in the Wesleyan schoolreom on Mon- day evening, when the commodious room was crowded. The title of the lecture was "The nameless prophet of Judah, and his wonderful history.." After the singing of a hymn, the Rev. J. Kirtlan said it was a grand finish to a day remarkable for the opening of the "Ship Canal to have a lecture from the Rev. P. Mackenzie. He added that owing to the illness of Mr. Hartley, who was to have been chairman, that position would be filled by Mr Henshall. Mr. Henshall in referring to the lecturer, said time seemed to deal very gentle with him. Mr. Mackenzie, who received a most enthusiastic reception, dealt very exhaustively with the history of Jeroboam, and the incidents conmected with his reign over the. Draelites,dwelling at length on the visits of the unknown prophet to Jeroboam. The lecturer spoke of the pro- phet's disobedience, and his punishment which resulted in his death. The Rev. Owen Watkins moved a vote of thanks to the lecturer and chairman ,which was seconded by the Rev. G. D. Mason. The Rev. J. Kirtlan sup- ported the motion, andit was carried with acclamation. ! A collection was taken on behalf of the Chapel Trust and Building Fund. ELLESMERE-ST. WESLEYAN MISSION. The first annual tea meeting in connection with the above mission took place on Tuesday night in the mis- sion room. About 100 persons sat down; s tea, after which a social gathering was held, over which the Rev. G. D. Mason presided. After singing and prayer, Mr. I. Barlow read the annual report, which stated that last year they were without premises in which to carry on their work,their tenancy of Buttermarket-st.school hav- ing expired, but by the kindness of the members of the Gospel Temperance Mission, they were allowed the use of their room until another could be obtained. Four gentlemen subsequently undertook the responsibility of finding means to erect a suitable building, and, as most' ef the members of the Sunday school came from Elles- ' mere-st. quarter, it was decided to build in that neigh- I bourbon& The present building was erected and opened ! on April 3rd,at a cost of £2OO or £3oo,which they hoped to wipe out as soon as possible. 'They had already £2O promised for the next anniversary, and the ladies of the mission were preparing for a sale of work to be held early in the year. At the opening of the school they had 213 scholars, and How 288, an increase of 75, with an ' average attendance of 50 in the morning, and 151 Ba the afternoon. 'There was a competent staff of teachers, all of whom were members of society. A penny bank had been established, which had proved very successful, and there were also 60 inembere attending the society classes. The Saturday Evening Penny Concerts were fully appi e• elated by the people of the district. In addition to the ' above, it had been intended to commence classes during ! the week, to teach, writing, reading, arithmetic, and shorthand, arid there was a newsroom in connection with the mission at which newspapers and periodicals I could be read, and also games of draughts, Scc., indulged in.—The Chairman said that Eilesmere-st. was an ideal mission, for what a good many people said ought to be done was being done. He believed that in ' the future the .present building would give way to a large. handsome, and permanent structure. In reading of Methodism, ho was struck by the progress it was making in the world. In London, for instance,although the population had increased eightfold during the past ,100 years,the membership of the Methodist Church had increased tenfold, and what was true of London was true of a great many other centres. He was much etruck by a remark of the Rev. Thomas Coek, who said that from his experience, whenever a Methodist chapel was built it was always filled. He was also a great believer in work among children and he thought that religious influences should bs brought to bear upon them in their earliest cleys,so that they might take the place of the present workers in the coming generation.—The Rev. J.Kirtlan expressed his satisfaction that the mission had been so successful in its work, and also with the educe- eational work they had taken in hand. They were show- ing what could be done by a place in which there were no millionaires, and no timo to spare. He generally found that the best workers were those who had no time to spare.—During the evening the following programme was gone through :—Song, "To-morrow will be Friday," Mr. F. Fieldhouse ; song, "'The devoted apple," Miss F Beetle; Lencaehire recitation, "The census," Mr. G. J. YONGE

There was a large congregation in Wycliffe Church on Sunday evening to hear the Rev. J. Yonge preach on the subject" 1893 : Its events and experiences of mark and meaning." He took his text from the first verse of the 101st Psalm. He said 1893 kad in many respects been a remarkable year. It was long, indeed, since in this country we had had such a remarkable continuance of fine weather. The previous year was very•different. Out of 52 Sundays some 40 were wet. The 1893 Sundays —troin March right on to nearly October—had been invariably fine. It was a very pleasant coincidence that in starting the P.S. A., fine weather should break out en the first Sunday and keep company with the movement . .

until it had become so established a success that wet weather could not hinder it. Still the year had been a

trying one. Speaking of their own town, it might truly be said that Warrington had been very hard hit during 1893. It was beset by disease in the beginning and br distress in the ending. Last New Year's Day found them in the midst of an epidemic of smallpox, which from its commencement in the July previous, until the end of March last year kTized 598 persons, and carried 60 of them off in death. Much debate took place during the time of that epidemic as to the lack of fore- sight and promptitude on tho part of town officials by which the disease was allowed to make headway until

it became almost impossible of restraint. There might have been ground for that criticism, but it would not be difficult to find reasons for lsnieney of judgment. It was an event without parallel for a long nu tuber of years. Thu present officials had had no experience of combating the evil, and with a lawful desire to avoid expensive and burdensome outlay, they hesitated too long in taking what proved to be absolutely necessary steps. Consequently, the disease spreal with terrible rapidity, until the whole town lay under a general ban. If, how- ever, that epidemic had its lessons of prudent fore- thought and prompt action for official men, he thought most assuredl) it had equally its lessoen for the people generally. It was vain for sanitary authorities to fight an epidemic if the people in whose interests they fought carelessly contributed to the'spread of the plague. This was done in the small-pox epiaeinic very largely. Num- bers of people jeopardized not only their own health, but the health of their families and neighbours, and even of ' passers-by in the streets by their needless contact with the victims of the disease. It was capable of by no means difficult proof that that epidemic, which des- troyed not (ally health and in maw: cases life also, but the comfort, the business, and the industry of the town, and saddled the borough with a sum of L'9,000 of ex- penditure,' was largely propagated and promoted by sheer carelessness, by reckless violation ef common sense, as well as by wilful neglect of the advice and precept of medical authoritiss. He submitted that the greatest care should be exercised with regard to contagious diseases. Referring to the distress caused by the coal stoppage, he said the fact that some 5,000 people a day were fed by public subscriptions, for over three months was evidence of a very lamentable condition of distress, while it was also an evidence of a large amount of generous symi:athy on the part of many ef those who were in more favoured circurestaaces. It was to hirn, and he thought it must be to all, a cause for satisfaction that upon this occasion the work of relief was taken in hand by all sects arid parties in far more general and united fashion than had formerly been the case. Mr. Yonge then proceeded to point out the absolute need of thrift, SD that when the "evil days" came they might be able to Inapt and en- dure them in a noble independence of publicTbarity. It was vain for them to agitate for higher wages until they knew how to really profit. by their wages, and how to rightly use their leisure. Referring to the completion of the Ship Canal, Mr. Yonge said he believed that there were better days coming for Wartingtcm, and hoped that they at Wycliffe, would have their eyes open, and be reaoy for useful christian service. Mr. Yonge afterwards referred to the past year from a national standpoiat.

ST. PAUL'S CEIURCLI.

The watch night service at St. Paul's Church, was conducted by the Vicar, the Rev. H. V. Pigot, and the Rev. T. M. R. Young-Hughes, curate. There was a large congregati®n. The Vicar, selected for his text the words " Tract ye in the Lord for ever, for in the Lor,. Jehovah is everlasting strength." He said that there was a par- ticular solemnity attaching to these years in the last' decade of the 19th century. Since they had come into the nineties, each year as it passed by, rang the knell of another century. He had chosen the text, because it gave them an assurance of permanence amid all these changes, and because it contained a warning to them against being drawn away by the novelties in God was the same, and their religion, if it was to stand the test of time, must be exactly that which had stood the test of this century. They might bo warned by the unchangeableness of God against any new fashions in religion and in their service. It was a solemn time to him that he had now completed the third year of his ministry at St. Paul's, and he trusted they had so far by heir experience gained confidence in him, that he was not prone to hasty change. but would lead them in the old paths." The hymn " 0 God our help in agas past," was sung, and as the congregation left the church wish- ing each other" A Happy New Year," the bells were h(ard announcing the birth of the new year by a merry peal.

HOW TO LIVE LONG.

Miss Eliza Work is au old lady who will be one hundred years old if she lives six weeks longer, and she resides at llenrietta, N.Y., U.S.A. Having been interviewed by an American journalist, she gives the following recipe for the attainment of old age :—" The reason I have lived so long," says Miss Work "and kept always so well and hearty, is because I never drank tea or coffee, and, above all, never got married." The good lady evidently lives up ' to her name, for she says•she has done a nig day's wo, k every day for more than ninety years, and hopes to do ' a great many more. Miss Work's brother lived to be 101, and she is of opinion that he would have lived much • longer if he had never married. "People who marry and drink coffee and tea ought not to expect to live very long." •

A labourer named Martin, residing at Sunderland, while mad with drink, flung a number ot deadly missiles from a second-storey window on Monday evening at passers-by, and seriously injured three persons, one at least, it is supposed fatally. It is stated that Elizabeth Mapp, the unfortunate girl who was convicted of child murder four years ago, was on Thursday released from prison. The sentence of death—which of course there was never any intention to carry out—was cosautitttd to penal servitude for life.

• • THE TALLEST SOLDIER. The regiment of Belgian Grenadiers can boast (the ." 'Belgian News'" says) of p.ssessing en its muster roll thestallest soldier.in Belgium. His name is Charles Louis Becaus. Bela. twenty-one years of age, was a farm labourer before ,joining the army,. and is a native of faundelgem. a village in West Flanders. Ili exaet'i heighti,s one metre ninety-seven centimetres (six feet six!, and a half inches). He comes of a family of giar ts and giantesses, but his mother is only of ordinary stature. His father is two metres high (six feet eight inches). Of ) his eleven brothers .s,nd sisters, the shortest mel.- cures one metre ninety-two centimetres. No boot to tit him could be found in the regimental clothing depo!. He is allowed double rations on the recommendation of his colonel.

DEATHS THROUGH EXPOSURE. FAITHFUL DOGS. Two touching incidents of canine faithfulness are re- ported from the Oldham district. Oa Tuesday the body of a pauper was found in a field at Butler Green, Chad- derton, guarded by a fox terrier. The man had died from exposure, and the deg had watched by hi body ; and at the last, when cliscevered, jealously refused teal- low its removal. On the following day another pauper was found to have similarly succumbed to the weather— this time near Wrigley Head Bridge, Failsworth. Here also a faithful workhouse dog had guarded the remains throughout the long night, and was loudly barking to attract attention when the body was discovered. At the last it refused to leave the body of the poor fellow. Napoleon Bonaparte Bonaparte has two royal successors on the island of St. Helena. They are Dinizulu, the son and heir of Cetewayo, the Zulu King, and Undabuko,brother of the same monarch. The climate Of St. Helena obliges them to wear a blAnket or some heavy covering most of the time. It is doubtful whether they will ever leave their island prison alive. THE STRIKE RIOTS IN WALES. A VOLUNTEER REGIMENT DISARMED. The Welsh police have made another arrest in con— nection with the strike riots at Pontymister,ancl several warrants are issued for the arrests of the ringleaders. 'rho local volunteer regiment has been disarmed by the. authorities in consequence of shots having been heard. in the district. Considerable excitement prevails owing to the introduction of additional blacklegs. Work was resumed yesterday by the Scotchmen and North of Eng- land steelworkers under the protection of a strong polleo- staff . 410' FEATHERSTONE MINERS AND NEW YEAR'S DAY. • DISMISSAL OF ABSENTEE MINERS. The whole of the miners employed at Lord Masham's pit at Featherstone, where the recent rioting took place,- have ceased work in consequence of Mr. Holliday, the manager,having dismissed seventy-five men who absented themselves from work on New Year's Day. Mr. Cowes', representing the Yorkshire Miners' Association,on Thurs- thy paid a visit to Featherstone, but no settlement wag arrived at. The men who were not discharged resumed work on Thuisday, but they received notice to hand in their fourteen days' taetice. In the meantime the offlcialg ,at Barnsley will try to settle the dispute.
